ox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi_{1}\lor\phi_{2}}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Left=Case}\\
\frac{\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\quad\text{FV}(\eta)\cap\text{MV}(c)=\emptyset}{\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi\land\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi\land\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Left=Const}\\
\frac{\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\quad\text{FV}(\eta)\cap\text{MV}(c)=\emptyset\quad\text{FV}(\psi)\subseteq T\cup\text{RV}(c)\cup\text{WV}(c)\quad\models\phi\to\langle T\cup\text{RV}(c)\rangle}{\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi\mathrel{\ast}\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi\mathrel{\ast}\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Left=Frame}

Figure 3
.
LINA
rules: from PSL.

\frac{\eta\in\text{CC}\quad\models_{\mathbf{Mem}}\eta\to\bigvee_{\alpha\in S}\eta_{\alpha}\quad\psi\in\text{CM}\quad\forall\alpha\in S.~\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi\mathrel{\ast}\eta_{\alpha}}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}}{\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi\mathrel{\ast}\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Left=RCase}\\
\frac{\models\phi\to\langle\text{RV}(c)\rangle\quad\text{FV}(\eta)\cap\text{MV}(c)=\emptyset\quad X\subseteq\text{RV}(c)\setminus\text{MV}(c)\quad y\notin\text{FV}(\eta)\quad\quad\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{y\sim f(X)}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\quad\textrm{$f$ is a monotone function}}{\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi\mathrel{\circledast}\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\langle y\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Left=NegFrame}\\
\frac{\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{{ev_{1}}={1}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[ev_{2}]\leq\delta}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}}{\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[ev_{1}]\geq 1-\epsilon}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[ev_{2}]\leq\delta+\epsilon}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Left=ProbBound}

Figure 4
.
LINA
rules: new and extended.

Next, we present the proof system of
LINA
. Since our assertions are a
conservative extension of assertions from PSL, most of the rules carry over
unchanged; we list existing rules in
Figure

3
. Here, we
comment on the new and generalized rules, which we list in

Figure

4
.

NA frame rule.

Our most important addition is the frame rule for the negative association
conjunction
\mathrel{\circledast}
. Informally, the
NegFrame
rule says that if a
set of variables
X
is negatively associated with another set of variables

Y
that satisfy
\eta
in a program state, and the program
c
performs a
monotone operation
f
on
X
and stores the result in a variable
y
, then in
the resulting program state,
y
and the untouched variables
Y
will also be
negatively associated, and
Y
will still satisfy
\eta
. Like the

Frame
rule for independence
\mathrel{\ast}
, the
NegFrame
rule
uses syntactic restrictions to control which variables the program may read
and write. The three sets of variables
\text{RV}(c),\text{WV}(c),\text{MV}(c)
represent the
variables that
c
may read from, must write to, and may modify, respectively;
these sets can be defined by induction on the syntax of the program. Roughly,
the side conditions guarantee the program
c
does not read from or modify
Y
,
the set of variables satisfying
\eta
; they in addition guarantee that
X
,
the domain of the monotone map will not be modified by
c
, and
y
, the
codomain of the monotone map does not belong to
Y
.

Generalized random case analysis.

As a more minor extension, we also generalize the randomized case analysis
rule from PSL in
RCase
. At a high level, this rule allows reasoning
by case analysis on a property
\eta
of the program memory (e.g., whether a
variable is true or false). Since the input is a distribution, which may have
some probability of
\eta
holding, and some probability of
\eta
not
holding, soundness of the rule is a delicate matter requiring several
technical side conditions. The original rule in PSL only allowed case analysis
on a Boolean expression; we generalize this rule to allow a case analysis on
any finite number of cases (e.g., performing case analysis on the value of a
bounded variable).

To explain this rule, we first introduce the side conditions in order. We say
that a formula
\eta
is
closed under conditioning
(CC) if for any

(\sigma,\mu)\models\eta
, for any
m
in the support of
\mu
,

(\sigma,\delta(m))\models\eta
. In the second condition,
\models_{\mathbf{Mem}}\phi
denotes that for any
\sigma\in\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}]
,
m\in\mathbf{Mem}[T]
where

T\subseteq\mathcal{RV}
,
(\sigma,\delta(m))\models\phi
, which says

\phi
is valid on all effectively deterministic configurations. Finally, we say
that a formula
\phi
is
closed under mixtures
(CM) if
(\sigma,\mu_{1})\models\phi
,
(\sigma,\mu_{2})\models\phi
and
\mu
is a convex
combination of
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
together imply
(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})\models\phi
.

Then, the rule
RCase
says if an assertion
\eta
is independent from
the rest of the assertions in the pre-condition,
\eta
is closed under
conditioning, and the post-condition
\psi
is closed under mixtures, then we
can perform case analysis on
\eta
to derive
\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi\mathrel{\ast}\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}
. Intuitively, every memory
m
in the support of the input
memory distribution satisfies
\eta_{a}
for some case
a\in S
. The main premise shows that
the output distribution of program
c
from any such input
m
satisfies
\psi
. Then,
since any distribution
\mu
on inputs is a convex combination of
such memories
m
, and
\psi
holds on each conditional output distribution,
we have
\psi
holds on the entire output distribution by convex closure.

Bounding bad events.

In addition, we present the rule
ProbBound
to facilitate bounding
tail probabilities. It says that if the pre-condition
{ev_{1}}={1}

guarantees that event
ev_{2}
happens for at most
\delta
probability
after command
c
, then in general, event
ev_{2}
happens for at most
probability
\delta+\epsilon
after
c
, where
\epsilon
upper bounds the
probability that
ev_{1}
is not true in the pre-condition. The validity of
this rule uses the law of total probability, which says for any two events

ev_{1}
and
ev_{2}
,

\displaystyle\Pr(ev_{1})

\displaystyle=\Pr(ev_{1}\mid ev_{2})\cdot\Pr(ev_{2})+\Pr(ev_{1}\mid\neg ev_{2})\cdot\Pr(\neg ev_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\Pr(ev_{1}\mid ev_{2})+\Pr(\neg ev_{2}).

As expected, the
LINA
proof system is sound.

Theorem 5.11.

(Soundness of
LINA
)
If
\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}
is derivable,
then it is valid:
\models\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}
.

6.
Examples

Now that we have introduced
LINA
, we present a series of formalized
case studies. Our examples are extracted from various algorithms using hashing
and balls-into-bins processes.

6.1.
Preliminaries: probabilities, expectations, concentration bounds

Our examples will use a handful of standard facts about probability
distributions, encoded as axioms in the assertion logic. We will
generally mention these axioms before they are used, but here we
introduce one fact that we will use through all of our examples: the

Chernoff bound
.

In each of our examples we will establish negative
dependence of a sequence of random variables
\{X_{i}\}_{i}
and apply a

concentration bound
: a theorem showing that the sum
X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n}

is usually close to its expected value. This kind of analysis is useful for
establishing
high-probability guarantees
of randomized algorithms, e.g.,
showing that the error of a random estimate is at most
0.01
with probability
at least
99\%
.

Theorem 6.1 (Chernoff bound for NA variables
(
Dubhashi and
Ranjan 1998
)
).

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be a sequence of NA random variables, each bounded in

[0,1]
, and let
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
. Then for any failure probability

\beta\in(0,1]
, we have:

\Pr[|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|\geq T(\beta,n)]\leq\beta\ \ \text{ where }T(\beta,n)=\sqrt{(n/2)\ln(2/\beta)}.

To hide complex numerical bounds, we use the notation
T(\beta,n)

for the above function throughout. In our assertion logic, the
Chernoff bound can be encoded as the following axiom schema:

Theorem 6.2 (Chernoff bound, axiom).

Let
\{x_{\alpha}\}
be a family of variables indexed by
\alpha
, where
each variable is bounded in
[0,1]
and is a monotone function of its program
variables. Then for any
\beta\in(0,1]
, the following axiom schema is
sound in our model:

(NA-Chernoff)

\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle x_{\alpha}\rangle\to\Pr\left[\ \middle|\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}x_{\alpha}-\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}x_{\alpha}\right]\middle|\ \geq T(\beta,n)\right]\leq\beta

We will also use a new expression in our assertions:
\mathbb{E}[f]
, where
f
is a
non-negative and bounded numeric expression, denotes the expected value of
f

in the current program configuration. We also observe the following conventions
throughout the examples: logical variables are denoted by Greek (
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\dots
) and capital Roman letters (
M,N,K,\dots
). Program variables
start with lower-case Roman letters (
x,y,z,\dots
).

6.2.
Bloom filter, high-level

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsc{Bloom}:\\
\quad{bloom}\leftarrow{zero(N)};\\
\quad{m}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\mathbf{while}\ m<M\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad{h}\leftarrow{0}\\
\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ h<H\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad{bin}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-0.75346pt}[0.75346pt]{\tiny$\mathdollar$}}}{{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}[0.86108pt]{$\leftarrow$}}}{oh([N])};\\
\quad\quad\quad{upd}\leftarrow{bloom\mathop{||}bin};\\
\quad\quad\quad{bloom}\leftarrow{upd};\\
\\
\\
\\
\quad\quad\quad{h}\leftarrow{h+1};\\
\quad\quad{m}\leftarrow{m+1};\end{array}

(a)

Higher-level version

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsc{BloomArray}:\\
\quad{bloom}\leftarrow{zero(N)};\\
\quad{m}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\mathbf{while}\ m<M\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad{h}\leftarrow{0}\\
\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ h<H\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad{bin}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-0.75346pt}[0.75346pt]{\tiny$\mathdollar$}}}{{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}[0.86108pt]{$\leftarrow$}}}{oh([N])};\\
\quad\quad\quad{n}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ n<N\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{upd}\leftarrow{bloom[n]\mathop{||}bin[n]};\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{bloom[n]}\leftarrow{upd};\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{n}\leftarrow{n+1}\\
\quad\quad\quad{h}\leftarrow{h+1};\\
\quad\quad{m}\leftarrow{m+1}\end{array}

(b)

Array version

Figure 5
.
Bloom filter examples

Next, we revisit the Bloom filter example introduced in
Section

2
. We
show how to translate the informal argument in
Section

2
into formal
proofs in our program logic. First, we will analyze the process of adding items
into a Bloom filter
bloom
and prove that the entries in
bloom
are negatively
associated at the end of the process. Second, we will analyze a program that
checks the membership of a new item in a given Bloom filter and show how
to bound its false positive rate. Last, we combine them together into one proof
that bounds the false positive rate of a Bloom filter with
M
elements.

Proving NA of
bloom

We reproduce the code for
Bloom
in
Figure

5(a)
.
This program is a higher-level version of the program in
Figure

5(b)
,
which performs array operations bit-by-bit.
We align the two versions so that the equivalent operations are side-by-side.
We will demonstrate our program logic on the higher-level version first and
analyze the array version later in
Section

6.3
.

Recall that the code models inserting
M
distinct elements into a Bloom filter
backed by an array
bloom
of length
N
, where each element is hashed by
H

functions, each producing an element of
[N]
uniformly at random.
We refer to the outer loop as
\mathit{outer}
, and the inner
loop as
\mathit{inner}
. For both the outer and the inner loop, we apply the
rule
Loop
with the loop invariant:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle
.
We consider the inner loop first. We show that the invariant is preserved by the
body of
\mathit{inner}
. After the
oh
sampling command,
RSamp*
gives:

\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\right)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{OH}_{[N]}\langle bin\rangle

By negative association of the one-hot distribution (
OH-PNA
), we get

\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\right)\mathrel{\ast}\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{N}bin[\gamma]\right)

which implies

\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\right)\mathrel{\circledast}\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{N}bin[\gamma]\right)

using
Weak
. Rearranging terms, this is equivalent to

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\langle bin[\beta]\rangle.

After the assignment to
upd
, we have:

\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\langle bin[\beta]\rangle\right)\land{upd}\sim{bloom\mathop{||}bin}.

Because
\mathop{||}
is monotone, applying the monotone mapping axiom
(
Mono-Map
) gives us:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle upd[\beta]\rangle.

Using the assignment rule (
RAssn
) on the assignment to
\mathit{bloom}

shows that the loop invariant is preserved by the inner loop. Thus,

Loop
gives:

\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\mathit{inner}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

Next, we turn to the outer loop. The argument showing that the invariant is
preserved by the outer loop follows by a straightforward argument, since the
outer loop only modifies
bloom
through the inner loop, so
Loop
gives:

\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\mathit{outer}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

Then, we have:

\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\top}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{Bloom}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

because initializing
bloom
to the all-zeros vector, a deterministic value,
establishes the loop invariant. This judgment shows that the
bloom
vector
satisfies NA at the end of the program.

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsc{CheckMem}(H,bloom):\\
\quad{h}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad{allhit}\leftarrow{1}\\
\quad\mathbf{while}\ h<H\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad{bin}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-0.75346pt}[0.75346pt]{\tiny$\mathdollar$}}}{{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}[0.86108pt]{$\leftarrow$}}}{\mathbf{U}_{[N]}};\\
\quad\quad{hit}\leftarrow{bloom[bin]};\\
\quad\quad{allhit}\leftarrow{hit\mathop{\&\&}allhit};\\
\quad\quad{h}\leftarrow{h+1};\end{array}

Figure 6
.
Check the membership of a new item

Bounding the false positive rate

Now, we turn to verifying a bound on the false positive rate of the Bloom
filter. Recall that a false positive occurs if when querying with an element
that was not inserted, the filter returns true. We can encode the membership
check of a new element as a program
CheckMem

(H,bloom)
, listed
in
Figure

6
, which hashes the new element into
H
uniformly
random positions and checks if these positions are all set to one in the filter.
If so, the Bloom filter will report that the new element is in set, when it was
never inserted—a false positive.

To verify the false positive rate, we place the program
\textsc{CheckMem}(H,bloom)

immediately after
Bloom
, and then verify a bound on the probability
that
allhit
is 1 at the end of the combined program. We first apply the
Chernoff bound to the NA variables (
NA-Chernoff
) to prove that, with
high probability, the number of occupied bins in
Bloom
is near its mean
with high probability:

\Bigg\{\top\Bigg\}{\textsc{Bloom}}\Bigg\{\Pr\left[\ \middle|\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]-\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]\right]\middle|\ \geq T(\delta,N)\right]\leq\delta\Bigg\}.

This concentration bound implies that a tail bound, which says with high
probability
\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]
is upper bounded by its expected
value plus
T(\delta,N)
,

(2)

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\top\Bigg\}{\textsc{Bloom}}\Bigg\{\Pr\left[\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]<\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]\right]+T(\delta,N)\right]\geq 1-\delta\Bigg\}.

Then we analyze
CheckMem
and show in

Appendix

F

that

\displaystyle\Big\{\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]<K\Big\}{\textsc{CheckMem}}\Big\{\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{H}\Big\}.

Then, by the
ProbBound
rule and basic axioms about probabilities,
we have

(3)

\displaystyle\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]<K]\geq 1-\delta}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{CheckMem}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{H}+\delta}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

We then use
Seqn
to combine the proved judgements for
Bloom

(
2
) and
CheckMem
(
3
) to derive that, for any

\delta
,

\displaystyle\hbox{\raisebox{-7.00009pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1048582}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\top}\hbox{\raisebox{-7.00009pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1048582}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{Bloom};\textsc{CheckMem}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-7.00009pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1048582}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]\big]+T(\delta,N)}{N}\right)^{H}+\delta}\hbox{\raisebox{-7.00009pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1048582}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Since
allhit
is 1 exactly when there is a false positive, this judgment
proves an upper bound on the false positive rate of the Bloom filter.
5
5

5

The precise expected value is
N\cdot(1-(1-1/N)^{M\cdot H})
, a fact
which can also be shown in our logic. Roughly speaking, this fact follows
because each element of
bloom
is the logical-or of
M\cdot H

probabilistically independent bits, each
1
with probability
1/N
and
0

otherwise. This argument does not rely on negative association.

6.3.
Bloom filter, low-level

The previous Bloom filter uses a vector operation
bloom\mathop{||}bin
to
transform an array of negatively associated values. We next consider a
lower-level version of the previous example,
BloomArray
, in

Figure

5(b)
, where the vector operation is replaced by a loop that
applies the Boolean-or.

Let
\mathit{outer}
and
\mathit{mid}
be the outer-most and second outer-most
loops, and let
\mathit{inner}
be the inner-most loop. Again, our goal is to
show that the vector
bloom
is negatively associated at the end of the program.
We first prove the following judgment for
\mathit{inner}
:

\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{N}\langle bin[\gamma]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\mathit{inner}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=n}^{N}\langle bin[\gamma]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

We will apply the rule
Loop
on
\mathit{inner}
with the following loop
invariant:

\phi=\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=k}^{N}\langle bin[\gamma]\rangle

To show that the loop invariant is preserved by the body, we can first show:

\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\langle bloom[n],bin[n]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{upd}\leftarrow{bloom[n]\mathop{||}bin[n]}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{{upd}\sim{bloom[n]\mathop{||}bin[n]}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

using
RAssn
. Noting that the boolean-or operator is a monotone operation, we
may apply the frame rule
NegFrame
to obtain:

\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\langle bloom[n],bin[n]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{upd}\leftarrow{bloom[n]\mathop{||}bin[n]}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\langle upd\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

with the framing condition

\eta=\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{n}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\right)\mathrel{\circledast}\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=n+1}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\right)\mathrel{\circledast}\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=n+1}^{N}\langle bin[\gamma]\rangle\right).

Thus, by re-associating the separating conjunction and applying
RAssn

for the remaining two assignments in the inner-most loop, we have:

\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{upd}\leftarrow{bloom[n]\mathop{||}bin[n]};{bloom[n]}\leftarrow{upd};{n}\leftarrow{n+1}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

and thus by
Loop
, we have:

\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=n}^{N}\langle bin[\gamma]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\mathit{inner}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=n}^{N}\langle bin[\gamma]\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.51782pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1867229}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Now for loop
\mathit{mid}
, we establish the same loop invariant as we took
before:

\psi=\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{N}\langle bloom[\beta]\rangle

If
\psi
holds at the beginning of
\mathit{mid}
, then invariant for the
inner-most loop
\phi
holds after assigning
0
to
n
and sampling
bin
,
since
bin
is independent of
\psi
(
RSamp*
) and
bin
is distributed
as
oh(n)
, which implies entries in
bin
are negatively associated
(
OH-PNA
). Furthermore,
\phi
implies
\psi
at the exit of

\mathit{inner}
, by dropping the conjunct describing
bin
. Thus,
\psi
is a
valid invariant for
\mathit{mid}
, and the rest of the proof proceeds
unchanged.

6.4.
Permutation hashing

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsc{PermHash}:\\
\quad{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-0.75346pt}[0.75346pt]{\tiny$\mathdollar$}}}{{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}[0.86108pt]{$\leftarrow$}}}{perm([B\cdot K])};\\
\quad{n}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad{ct}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\mathbf{while}\ n<N\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad{bin[n]}\leftarrow{mod(g[n],B)};\\
\quad\quad{hitZ[n]}\leftarrow{[bin[n]=Z]};\\
\quad\quad{ct}\leftarrow{ct+hitZ[n]};\\
\quad\quad{n}\leftarrow{n+1}\end{array}

Figure 7
.
Permutation hashing

Our second example considers a scheme for hashing using a random permutation.
Consider the program in
Figure

7
, from an algorithm for fast set
intersection
(
Ding and
König 2011
)
. Letting
B
be the number of
bins, and the data universe be
[B\cdot K]=\{1,\dots,B\cdot K\}
where

B\cdot K\geq N
, we
first draw a uniformly random permutation
g
of the data universe. Then, we
hash the numbers
n\in[N]
into
bin[n]
by applying the hash function
g
and
then taking the result modulo
B
. Then, we record whether the item landed in a
specific bucket
Z
by computing the indicator
hitZ[n]=[bin[n]=Z]
, which
is
1
if
bin[n]=Z
and
0
otherwise, and accumulate the result into the
count
ct
.

Our goal is to show that
ct
is usually not far from its expected value, which
is
N/B
. If the quantities
\{[bin[n]=Z]\}_{n}
were independent, we would be
able to apply a standard concentration bound to the sum
ct
. However,

\{bin[n]=Z\}_{n}
are
not
independent: for instance, since exactly

K
elements from
[B\cdot K]
map to
Z
, if
bin[n]=Z
for
n\in\{0,1,\dots,K-1\}
, then
bin[K]=Z
must be false.

Nevertheless, we can show that
\{[bin[n]=Z]\}_{n}
are negatively associated
random variables. Intuitively,
\{g[n]\}_{n}
are NA random variables because
the result of a uniformly random permutation is NA. Then,
\{bin[n]\}_{n}
is
computed by mapping the function
mod(-,B)
over the array
g
; since this
produces another uniform permutation distribution, the vector
\{bin[n]\}_{n}

is also NA. By similar reasoning
\{[bin[n]=Z]\}_{n}
is also NA, as it is
obtained by mapping the function
[-\mathrel{=}Z]
over
\{bin[n]\}_{n}
.
Since this example is similar to the first Bloom filter example, except applying
the negative association of the permutation distribution (
Perm-PNA
) and
the permutation map axiom (
Perm-Map
), we defer the details to the
appendix.

6.5.
Fully-dynamic dictionary

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsc{FDDict}:\\
\quad{binCt}\leftarrow{zero(C,P)};\\
\quad{n}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\mathbf{while}\ n<N\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad{crate[n]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-0.75346pt}[0.75346pt]{\tiny$\mathdollar$}}}{{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}[0.86108pt]{$\leftarrow$}}}{oh([C])};\\
\quad\quad{pocket[n]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-0.75346pt}[0.75346pt]{\tiny$\mathdollar$}}}{{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}[0.86108pt]{$\leftarrow$}}}{oh([P])};\\
\quad\quad{bin[n]}\leftarrow{crate[n]^{\top}\cdot pocket[n]};\\
\quad\quad{p}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ p<P\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad{c}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ c<C\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{upd}\leftarrow{binCt[c][p]+bin[n][c][p]};\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{binCt[c][p]}\leftarrow{upd};\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{c}\leftarrow{c+1};\\
\quad\quad\quad{p}\leftarrow{p+1};\\
\quad\quad{n}\leftarrow{n+1};\\
\quad{p}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\mathbf{while}\ p<P\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad{c}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ c<C\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad{over[c][p]}\leftarrow{[binCt[c][p]>T_{bin}]};\\
\quad\quad\quad{upd}\leftarrow{overCt[c]+over[c][p]};\\
\quad\quad\quad{overCt[c]}\leftarrow{upd};\\
\quad\quad\quad{c}\leftarrow{c+1};\\
\quad\quad{p}\leftarrow{p+1}\end{array}

(a)

Fully-dynamic dictionary
(
Bercea and Even 2019
)

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsc{RepeatBIB}:\\
\quad{r}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\mathbf{while}\ r<R\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad{n}\leftarrow{0}\\
\quad\quad{rem}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ n<N\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad{ct[n]}\leftarrow{ct[n]-[ct[n]>0]};\\
\quad\quad\quad{rem}\leftarrow{rem+[ct[n]>0]};\\
\quad\quad\quad{n}\leftarrow{n+1};\\
\quad\quad{j}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ j<rem\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad{bin[j]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-0.75346pt}[0.75346pt]{\tiny$\mathdollar$}}}{{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}[0.86108pt]{$\leftarrow$}}}{oh([N])};\\
\quad\quad\quad{k}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ k<N\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{upd}\leftarrow{ct[k]+bin[j][k]};\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{ct[k]}\leftarrow{upd};\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{k}\leftarrow{k+1};\\
\quad\quad\quad{j}\leftarrow{j+1};\\
\quad\quad{n}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad{emptyCt[r]}\leftarrow{0};\\
\quad\quad{empty}\leftarrow{isZero(ct)};\\
\quad\quad\mathbf{while}\ n<N\ \mathbf{do}\\
\quad\quad\quad{upd}\leftarrow{emptyCt[r]+empty[n]};\\
\quad\quad\quad{emptyCt[r]}\leftarrow{upd};\\
\quad\quad\quad{n}\leftarrow{n+1};\\
\quad\quad{r}\leftarrow{r+1};\end{array}

(b)

Repeated balls-into-bins
(
Becchetti et al. 2019
)

Figure 8
.
Larger examples

For our next example, we consider a hashing scheme for a
fully-dynamic dictionary, a space-efficient data structure that supports
insertions, deletions, and membership queries. The top level of the
data structure by
Bercea and Even 2019
uses a two-level
hashing scheme: elements are first hashed into a
crate
, and then hashed
into a
pocket dictionary
within each crate. As part of the space
analysis of their scheme,
Bercea and Even 2019
proves a
high-probability bound on the number of pocket dictionaries that overflow after
a given number of elements are inserted.

We extract the program
FDDict
in
Figure

8(a)
from the
scheme in
Bercea and Even 2019
. The program models the
insertion of
N
elements. Each element is first hashed into one of
C
possible
crates uniformly at random, and then hashed into one of
P
possible pocket
dictionaries uniformly at random. The variable
bin[n]
is a
C
by
P
matrix,
with all entries zero except for the entry at
(crate[n],pocket[n])
, which is
set to
1
. Next, the program totals up the number of elements hashing to each
(crate, pocket) pair, storing the result in the
C
by
P
matrix
binCt
.
Finally, the program checks which (crate, pocket) pairs have count larger than
some concrete threshold
T_{bin}
(
over
), and totals up the number of full
pocket dictionaries in each crate (
overCt
).

Our logic can prove a judgment of the following form:

\bigg\{\top\bigg\}{\textsc{FDDict}}\bigg\{\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\Pr[overCt[\gamma]>P\cdot\rho_{bin}+T(\rho_{over},P)]\leq\rho_{over}\bigg\},

where the logical variables
\rho_{bin}
and
\rho_{over}
represents the
parametric overflow properties. This formalizes a result similar to

Bercea and Even 2019
, which states that except
with probability
\beta
, all crates have at most
T_{over}
overfull pocket
dictionaries. The core of the proof shows that for every crate index
\gamma
,
the counts
binCt[\gamma][\beta]
are negatively associated, using the

NegFrame
rule as in the array version of the Bloom filter example.
Then, we show that vector
over[\gamma][\beta]
, which indicates whether each
pocket dictionary
\beta
in crate
\gamma
is overfull or not, is also
negatively associated. This holds because
over[\gamma][\beta]
is obtained from

binCt[\gamma][\beta]
by applying a monotone function. Furthermore, the count
of overflows
overCt[\gamma]
is obtained by another monotone function on

over[\gamma][\beta]
and thus its entries are also negatively associated.

6.6.
Repeated balls-into-bins process

Our final example considers a probabilistic protocol proposed by

Becchetti et al. 2019
, implemented as
RepeatBIB
in

Figure

8(b)
. Intuitively, the program implements a repeated
balls-into-bins process. Initially,
N
balls are distributed among
N
bins
(
ct[n]
). For
R
rounds, in each round a ball is first removed from every
non-empty bin. Then, the
rem
removed balls are randomly reassigned to bins.
This process is useful for distributed protocols and scheduling algorithms,
where the balls represent tasks and the bins represent computation nodes.

Becchetti et al. 2019
proposed and analyzed this algorithm
(e.g., bounding the maximum load, proving how long it takes for all balls to
visit all bins). We can verify the following lower-bound on the number of empty
bins, analogous to
Becchetti et al. 2019
:

\Bigg\{N\geq 2\land{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}ct[\alpha]}\sim{N}\Bigg\}{\textsc{RepeatBIB}}\Bigg\{\Pr\left[\bigvee_{\beta=0}^{R}(emptyCt[\beta]<N/15-T(\rho_{empty},N))\right]\leq R\cdot\rho_{empty}\Bigg\}

Two aspects of this program make it more difficult to verify. First, there is
a loop with a randomized guard: the number of removed balls
rem
is randomized
quantity. Reasoning about such loops is challenging, because our
Loop

rule is not directly applicable and only far weaker rules are available for
loops with general randomized guards.
Becchetti et al. 2019

sidestep this problem by
conditioning
on the number of balls in each
bin, which also fixes
rem
to be some value, proving the target property for
every fixed setting, and then combining the proofs together.
LINA
can
formalize this style of reasoning using the randomized case analysis rule
(
RCase
) to condition on
rem
’s value, and then apply the

Loop
rule; however, the post-condition of
RCase
must be
closed under mixtures (CM), while independence and negative association are
known
not
to satisfy this side-condition. Thus, it is not possible to
prove negative association by first conditioning and then combining. To work
around this second problem, we use a technique from

Becchetti et al. 2019
and prove, on each conditional
distribution, a high-probability bound using the Chernoff bound. The
benefit of this approach is that high-probability bounds
are
CM, so we
can apply
RCase
to combine the results. In our view, the fact that

LINA
can handle this kind of subtle argument involving conditioning is
a strength of our approach.

7.
Related work

Bunched implications.

The logic of bunched implications (BI)
(
O’Hearn and Pym 1999
;
Pym 2002
)
is a well-studied
substructural logic. BI has a
resource semantics

(
Pym
et al. 2004
)
, where
states are resources and the separating conjunction combines compatible
resources together. We follow Docherty’s uniform presentation and
investigation of BI
(
Docherty 2019
)
; in particular, our negative
association model relies on Docherty’s non-deterministic frame conditions, and
we use his duality-theoretic framework to establish
M
-BI’s metatheory.

Separation logics.

The first separation logic was developed to verify pointer-manipulating
programs
(
Reynolds 2001
;
Ishtiaq and
O’Hearn 2001
;
O’Hearn
et al. 2001
)
. There is long line of work on
separation logic for concurrency, starting
from
(
O’Hearn 2007
;
Brookes 2007
)
and
continuing to the present day (e.g.,
(
Sergey
et al. 2015
;
Jung et al. 2018
)
).

More recently, separation logics have been
developed for probabilistic programs.
LINA
is an extension of
PSL
(
Barthe
et al. 2020
)
, a separation logic for probabilistic independence.

Bao
et al. 2021
propose DIBI, an extension of BI with a non-commutative
conjunction, and developed a program logic with DIBI assertions that is capable
of proving conditional independence.
Batz et al. 2019

propose QSL, a separation logic where assertions have a
quantitative

interpretation, and used their logic to verify probabilistic and
heap-manipulating programs.
Tassarotti and
Harper 2019
develop a separation logic for
relational reasoning about probabilistic programs, using the coupling approach
of pRHL
(
Barthe et al. 2012
)
.

Verifying approximate data structures and applying concentration bounds.

Bloom filters are a data structure supporting
approximate membership
queries
(AMQs). Ceramist
(
Gopinathan and
Sergey 2020
)
is a recent
framework for verifying hash-based AMQ structures in the Coq theorem prover.
Besides handling Bloom filters, Ceramist supports subtle proofs of correctness
for many other AMQs. Compared with our approach, Ceramist proofs are more
precise but also more intricate, applying theorems about Stirling numbers to
achieve a precise bound on the false positive probability. In contrast, our
approach reasons about negative dependence to achieve a substantially simpler
proof, albeit with less precise bounds.

Prior works in verification have also applied the Chernoff bound to bound sums
of independent random quantities (e.g.,
(
Wang et al. 2021
;
Chakarov and
Sankaranarayanan 2013
)
). While independence is easier to establish, the negative
association property that we need is more subtle.

Negative dependence.

There are multiple definitions of negative dependence in the literature, each
with their own strengths and weaknesses. We work with negative association
(NA)
(
Joag-Dev and
Proschan 1983
;
Dubhashi and
Ranjan 1998
)
, because it holds in many
situations where negative dependence should hold and it is closed under various
notions of composition. Recently, the notion of Strong Rayleigh (SR)
(
Borcea
et al. 2009
)

distribution has been proposed as an ideal definition of negative dependence.
The SR condition satisfies more closure properties than NA does; in particular,
it is preserved under various forms of conditioning. However, SR distributions
have mostly been studied for Boolean variables only, and we do not know if an
analogue of the monotone maps property of NA holds for SR.

Beyond theoretical investigations, negative dependence plays a useful role in
many practical applications. In machine learning, negative dependence can help
ensure diversity in predictions by a
model
(
Kulesza and
Taskar 2012
)
, and fast algorithms are known to
learn and sample from negatively-dependent
distributions
(
Anari
et al. 2016
)
. In algorithm design, negative
dependence is a useful tool to randomly round solutions of linear programs to
integral solutions
(
Srinivasan 2001
)
. Negative dependence can
ensure that certain constraints are satisfied exactly after rounding, while
still allowing concentration bounds to be applied to analyze the quality of the
rounded solution.

8.
Conclusion and future direction

We introduced
LINA
, a probabilistic program logic that can reason about
independence and negative association. Assertions in
LINA
are based on
a novel probabilistic model of
M
-BI, an extension of the logic of Bunched
Implications with multiple separating conjunctions. We demonstrated how to use

LINA
to reason about probabilistic hashing schemes, and a repeated
balls-into-bins process. There are several natural directions for future work.

Other models of
M
-BI, and non-deterministic frames.

The assertion logic
M
-BI was primarily motivated by our NA model, but it is
general enough that we believe there are likely other natural models. Exploring
these directions could allow modeling finer notions of separation, and could
further justify
M
-BI as an interesting logic in its own right. It would also
be interesting to see if there are other models that use a non-deterministic
operator to combine resources, as proposed by
Docherty 2019
.

Verifying negative association for sampling algorithms.

We used NA to analyze probabilistic hashing schemes. Another classical
application of NA is in
sampling schemes
, which generate a sample from a
target distribution while satisfying certain
constraints
(
Dubhashi
et al. 2007
;
Brändén and
Jonasson 2012
)
. NA
samplers are useful in algorithm design
(
Srinivasan 2001
)
and
statistics, and it would be interesting to understand how to verify these
programs. Many samplers employ rejection sampling, which is not easily analyzed
in
LINA
but which could be expressed with an explicit conditioning
operator, as in probabilistic programming
languages
(
Gordon
et al. 2014
)
.

We thank the anonymous reviewers for their helpful feedback and suggestions.
This work benefited from discussions with Simon Docherty. This work was
supported in part by the
Sponsor NSF
http://dx.doi.org/10.13039/100000001
under
Grant No. Grant #2035314, Grant #1943130, Grant #2040249, Grant #2040222
and Grant #2152831.

Appendix A
Preliminaries

Lemma A.1.

Say
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq N\}
where
S_{i}
are disjoint,

S=\cup\mathcal{S}
and
\mu\in\mathbf{Mem}[S]
,

Then,
S_{i}
are independent in
\mu

if and only if for any family of all monotone or all antitone functions

f_{i}:\mathbf{Mem}[S_{i}]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x)\right]=\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}[f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x)].

Proof.

The forward direction is straightforward.
The backward direction needs more careful analysis.
In general, zero correlation does not imply independence,
but here, we have the equality for all family of monotone or antitone functions,
so that suffices for independence.

We prove by induction on
\mathcal{T}=\{S_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq K\}
that for any family of
v_{i}\in\mathbf{Mem}[S_{i}]
,

(5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\left(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i}\right)\land\left(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right)\right]=\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right].

Case
|\mathcal{T}|=1
::

Say
\mathcal{T}=\{S_{j}\}
.
Since indicator functions
S_{i}<v_{i}
and
S_{i}\leq v_{i}

are both monotonically decreasing,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x=v_{j}\land(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x\leq v_{j}\land(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x<v_{j}\land(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i})\right]

(By
Equation

4
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x\leq v_{j}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x<v_{j}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x\leq v_{j}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x<v_{j}\right])\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x=v_{j}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right]

Case
|\mathcal{T}|>1
:

Let
S_{j}
be an element in
\mathcal{T}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i})\land(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x\leq v_{j}\land(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{S_{j}\}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i})\land(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i})\right]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x<v_{j}\land(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{S_{j}\}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i})\land(\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x\leq v_{j}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{S_{j}\}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x<v_{j}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{S_{j}\}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}x=v_{j}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{S_{j}\}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i}\right]\cdot\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x<v_{i}\right]

When
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{S}
,
Equation

5
implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\bigwedge_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mu}\left[\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}x=v_{i}\right]

for any
v_{i}
’s. Thus, components in
\mathcal{S}
are independent.
∎

A.1.
Coarsening

We prove some properties of coarsening.
In the following we will use an alternative definition of coarsening,
which will be shown to be equivalent to what we define in the main text.

Definition A.2 (Alternative definition of coarsening).

We first index any partition
\mathcal{S}
as
\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{|\mathcal{S}|}
.
Say
|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|=m
,
|\mathcal{S}|=n
.
We say
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
coarsens a partition
\mathcal{S}

there exists a function a
f:[m]\to\mathcal{P}([n])
such that
1)
\cup_{i\in[m]}f(i)=[n]
;
2) for any
i,j\in[m]
, either
i=j
or
f(i),f(j)
are disjoint;
3)
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[m]\}
.

Lemma A.3.

Let
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be two partitions. Then
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

coarsens
\mathcal{S}
according to
Definition

A.2
if and
only if
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
coarsens
\mathcal{S}
according to
Definition

4.6
.

Proof.

We index
\mathcal{S}
as
\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{n}
and

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
as
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{|m|}
.

Backward direction:
By that definition, we know
a) for any
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}=\cup\mathcal{R}
for some
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
;
b)
\cup\mathcal{S}=\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

We define the function
g:[m]\to\mathcal{P}([n])
as

g(i)=\{j\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}\}
.
This
g
would satisfies all the conditions required:

(1)

By substitution,
\cup_{i\in[m]}g(i)=\cup_{i\in[m]}\{j\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}\}=\cup_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\{j\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}\}
.
By b), for any
j\in[n]
,
\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Then by a) and that
\mathcal{S}
is a partition, if
s^{\prime}
covers any of
\mathcal{S}_{j}
, it must
covers all of
\mathcal{S}_{j}
,
then
\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
implies there exists
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}
.
Thus,
j\in\{j\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}\}\subseteq\cup_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\{j\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}\}
.
For any
j\not\in[n]
,
\mathcal{S}_{j}
is undefined,
so it is impossible that
\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
\cup_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\{j\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}\}=[n]
.

(2)

For any
k\in g(i)
,
\mathcal{S}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}
.
If
i\neq j
, then
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{j}
are disjoint since
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is a partition.
Thus,
\mathcal{S}_{k}\not\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{j}
, and
k\not\in g(j)
.
So for any
i\neq j
,
g(i),g(j)
are disjoint.

(3)

By substitution,

\displaystyle\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in g(i)\}\mid i\in[m]\}

\displaystyle=\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}\}\mid i\in[m]\}

\displaystyle=\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}\}\mid s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}.

Again, by a) and that
\mathcal{S}
is a partition, if
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
covers any part of of
\mathcal{S}_{j}
, it must
covers all of
\mathcal{S}_{j}
, so
\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}\}=s^{\prime}
.
Thus,
\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq s^{\prime}\}\mid s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Forward direction:
By 3), we know that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[m]\}
.
So for any
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, we have
s^{\prime}=\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}
, which is a subset of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
by construction. So we proved a).
Also,
\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\cup\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[m]\}=\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\mid i\in[m]\}
,
and by 1), that is equivalent to

\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in[n]\}
, which is equivalent to
\cup\mathcal{S}
.

∎

We can prove that coarsening commute with projections.

Lemma A.4.

Given a partition
\mathcal{S}=\{\mathcal{S}_{i}\}_{i}
and a set
X
,
let
\mathcal{S}_{X}=\{\mathcal{S}_{i}\cap X\mid\mathcal{S}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\}
.
For any
\mathcal{T}
coarsening
\mathcal{S}_{X}
,
there exists a coarsening
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{S}
such that

\mathcal{T}=\{\mathcal{S}_{i}\cap X\mid\mathcal{S}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}
;
conversely, for any
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
coarsening
\mathcal{S}
,
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{X}=\{\mathcal{S}_{i}\cap X\mid\mathcal{S}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}
,
we have
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{X}
coarsens
\mathcal{S}_{X}
.

Proof.

Forward direction:
By
Definition

A.2
, there exists a coarsening function
f
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle=\{\cup\{{(\mathcal{S}_{X})}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[|\mathcal{T}|]\}

\displaystyle=\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\cap X\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[|\mathcal{T}|]\}

\displaystyle=\{(\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\})\cap X\mid i\in[|\mathcal{T}|]\}

(where
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[|\mathcal{T}|]\}
)

\displaystyle=\{S^{\prime}\cap X\mid S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
has the same size as
\mathcal{T}
, so

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{\cup\{\mathcal{S}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|]\}
,
and thus
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
coarsens
\mathcal{S}
.

Backward direction:

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
coarsens
\mathcal{S}
, so there exists a coarsening function
f
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{\cup\{S_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|]\}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{X}

\displaystyle=\{(\cup\{S_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\})\cap X\mid i\in[|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|]\}

\displaystyle=\{\cup\{S_{j}\cap X\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|]\}

\displaystyle=\{\cup\{{S_{X}}_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|]\}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{X}
coarsens
\mathcal{S}_{X}
.

∎

Appendix B
The logic
M
-BI

Figure

9
gives a Hilbert-style proof system for
M
-BI. If
one erases the subscripts on
\mathrel{\ast}
and
\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}
, then the rules without the
last three form a Hilbert proof system for BI.

\frac{~}{P\vdash P}\par\frac{~}{P\vdash\top}\quad\textsc{right = $\top$}\par\frac{~}{\bot\vdash P}\quad\textsc{right = $\bot$}\par\frac{P\vdash R\quad Q\vdash R}{P\lor Q\vdash R}\quad\textsc{right=$\lor$-E}\par\frac{P\vdash Q_{i}}{P\vdash Q_{1}\lor Q_{2}}\quad\textsc{right=$\lor$-I}\par\frac{P\vdash Q\quad P\vdash R}{P\vdash Q\land R}\quad\textsc{right=$\land$-I1}\par\frac{Q\vdash R}{P\land Q\vdash R}\quad\textsc{right=$\land$-I2}\par\frac{P\vdash Q_{1}\land Q_{2}}{P\vdash Q_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=$\land$-E}\par\frac{P\land Q\vdash R}{P\vdash Q\rightarrow R}\quad\textsc{right=$\rightarrow$-I}\par\frac{P\vdash Q\rightarrow R\quad P\vdash Q}{P\vdash R}\quad\textsc{right=$\rightarrow$-E}\par\frac{P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q\vdash R}{P\vdash Q\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}R}\quad\textsc{right=$\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}$}\par\frac{P\vdash Q\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}RS\vdash Q}{P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}S\vdash R}\quad\textsc{right=$\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}$-E}\par\frac{~}{P\dashv\vdash P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}I_{m}}\quad\textsc{right=$\mathrel{\ast}$-{Unit}}\par\frac{P\vdash R\quad Q\vdash S}{P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q\vdash R\mathrel{\ast}_{m}S}\quad\textsc{right=$\mathrel{\ast}$-{Conj}}\par\frac{~}{P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q\vdash Q\mathrel{\ast}_{m}P}\quad\textsc{right=$\mathrel{\ast}$-{Comm}}\par\frac{~}{(P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q)\mathrel{\ast}_{m}R\dashv\vdash P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}(Q\mathrel{\ast}_{m}R)}\quad\textsc{right= $\mathrel{\ast}$-{Assoc}}\par\frac{m_{1}\leq m_{2}}{P\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}Q\vdash P\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}Q}\quad\textsc{right= $\mathrel{\ast}$-{Inclusion}}\par

Figure 9
.
Hilbert system for
M
-BI

Using the M-BI rules, we can derive the rule
Cut
:

P\vdash Q

Q\vdash R

\land 2

P\land Q\vdash R

\rightarrow

P\vdash Q\rightarrow R

Cut

P\vdash R

See
3.1

Proof.

Let
m_{1}\leq m_{2}
. For the first rule, it suffices to show that
(P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m_{2}}Q)\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}P\vdash Q
. By

\mathrel{\ast}
-
Weakening
, we have that
(P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m_{2}}Q)\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}P\vdash(P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m_{2}}Q)\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}P
so the
result follows from
Cut
and
\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}-\textsc{E}
.

For the second rule,
\mathrel{\ast}_{1}
-
Unit
implies
I_{m_{2}}\vdash I_{m_{2}}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}I_{m_{1}}
;

\mathrel{\ast}
-
Weakening
implies
I_{m_{2}}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}I_{m_{1}}\vdash I_{m_{2}}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}I_{m_{1}}
;
and
\mathrel{\ast}_{2}
-
Unit
implies
I_{m_{2}}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}I_{m_{1}}\vdash I_{m_{1}}.

Then by
Cut
, we have
I_{m_{2}}\vdash I_{m_{1}}

∎

Appendix C
The Model of Negative Dependence and Independence

C.1.
A BI model for negative association

See
4.9

Proof.

Let
S
denote
\mathbf{dom}(\mu_{1})
and
T
denote
\mathbf{dom}(\mu_{2})
.

For any
\mu\in\mu_{1}\oplus_{s}\mu_{2}
, we have

\pi_{S}\mu=\mu_{1}
,

\pi_{T}\mu=\mu_{2}
, and
\mu
satisfies NA.

\mu
being NA
implies
\mu
is
\mathcal{R}
-
PNA
for any partition
\mathcal{R}
on

\mathbf{dom}(\mu)

So for any partition
\mathcal{S}
on
S
, partition
\mathcal{T}
on
T
,

\mu
is
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
.
Therefore,
\mu\in\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}
.

For any
\mu\in\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}
,

\pi_{S}\mu=\mu_{1}
,

\pi_{T}\mu=\mu_{2}
,
and
\mu
is
\{S,T\}
-
PNA
since
\mu_{1}
is
\{S\}
-
PNA
,

\mu_{2}
is
\{T\}
-
PNA
.
Thus,
\mu\in\mu_{1}\oplus_{w}\mu_{2}
.
∎

See
4.11

Proof.

We sketch the conditions, using the notation from the definition:

Down-Closed.:

Let
dom(x)=S,dom(x^{\prime})=S^{\prime},dom(y)=T,dom(y^{\prime})=T^{\prime}
. We claim that we
can take
z^{\prime}=\pi_{S^{\prime}\cup T^{\prime}}z
. We evidently have
z\sqsupseteq z^{\prime}
,
and
\pi_{S^{\prime}}z^{\prime}=\pi_{S^{\prime}}\pi_{S}z=x^{\prime}
and
\pi_{T^{\prime}}z^{\prime}=\pi_{T^{\prime}}\pi_{T}z=y^{\prime}
.

What remains to show is that
z^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
for any
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{T}

such that
x^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
,
y^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
,
and
(\cup\mathcal{S})\cap(\cup\mathcal{T})=\emptyset
.

If
x^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
, then
x
is
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
;
if
y^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
, then
y
is
\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
;
then
z\in x\oplus y
must be
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
.
Since
z^{\prime}:=\pi_{S^{\prime}\cup T^{\prime}}z
, and
(\cup\mathcal{S})\cup(\cup\mathcal{T})\subseteq S^{\prime}\cup T^{\prime}
, we have
z^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
too.
And evidently,
dom(z^{\prime})=S^{\prime}\cup T^{\prime}=dom(x^{\prime})\cup dom(y^{\prime})
.
So
z^{\prime}\in x^{\prime}\oplus y^{\prime}
.

Commutativity.:

Immediate.

Associativity.:

Let
dom(x)=R,dom(y)=S,dom(z)=T
. We can assume that these sets
are all disjoint, otherwise there is nothing to prove. We claim that we
can take
s=\pi_{S\cup T}w
.
For any
w
in
t\oplus z
,
t\in x\oplus y
,
we want to show that
w\in x\oplus s
and
s\in y\oplus z
.

•:

For any partition
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}
such that

(\cup\mathcal{R})\cap(\cup\mathcal{S})=\emptyset

and
x
is
\mathcal{R}
-
PNA
,
s
is
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
.
For set
X\in\mathbf{Var}
,
write
\{Y\cap X\mid Y\in\mathcal{S}\}
as
\mathcal{S}_{X}
.
Then, by
Lemma

A.4
,

s
is
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
implies
y
must be
\mathcal{S}_{S}
-
PNA
.
Similarly,

s
is
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
implies
z
must be
\mathcal{S}_{T}
-
PNA
.

Then,
t\in x\oplus y
must be
\mathcal{R}\cup(\mathcal{S}_{S})
-
PNA
,
and
w\in t\oplus z
must be

\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{S}_{S}\cup\mathcal{S}_{T}
-
PNA
.
Note that
\mathcal{S}
coarsens
\mathcal{S}_{S}\cup\mathcal{S}_{T}

so
w
is
\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{S}_{S}\cup\mathcal{S}_{T}
-
PNA
implies that
w
is
\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
.

Also,
\pi_{R}w=\pi_{R}\pi_{R\cup S}w=\pi_{R}t=x
,
and
dom(w)=R\cup S\cup T=dom(x)\cup dom(s)
.

Hence,
w\in x\oplus s
.

•:

Note that
x
is trivially
\{R\}
-
PNA
.
Then, for any partition
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T}
such that

R\cap(\cup\mathcal{S})\cap(\cup\mathcal{T})=\emptyset

and
y
is
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
and
z
is
\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
,
first
t
must be
(\{R\}\cup\mathcal{S})
-
PNA
,
and then
w
must be
(\{R\}\cup\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T})
-
PNA
.
By projection,
s=\pi_{S\cup T}
must be
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}z
-
PNA
.

Also,
\pi_{S}s=\pi_{S}\pi_{S\cup T}w=\pi_{S}w=\pi_{S}\pi_{R\cup S}w=\pi_{S}t=y
,
and similarly,
\pi_{T}s=z
.
Also,
dom(s)=S\cup T=dom(y)\cup dom(z)
.

Hence,
s\in y\oplus z
.

Unit Existence.:

Take
e
to be
\mu
where
\mu
is the (unique) distribution in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[\emptyset])
.

Unit Closure.:

Immediate as we take
E=M
.

Unit Coherence.:

Immediate:
x\in y\oplus e
entails
y=\pi_{dom(y)}x
, which implies
y\sqsubseteq x
.
∎

Theorem C.1.

Given a set of variables
S
,
S
satisfies NA in
\mu

iff
\mu
satisfies
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
for any

\mathcal{S}
partitioning
S
iff
\mu
satisfies
\{\{s\}\mid s\in S\}
-
PNA
.

Proof.

The second equivalence is straightforward:

•

\{\{s\}\mid s\in\mathcal{S}\}
is a partition of
S
, so we have the backward direction.

•

Any
\mathcal{S}

partitioning
S
coarsens
\{\{s\}\mid s\in\lx@sectionsign\}
, so we have the first direction.

For the forward direction of the first equivalence,
it suffices to prove that for any partition
\mathcal{S}
of
S
,
any family of all monotone or all antitone functions

f_{i}:\mathbf{Mem}[S_{i}]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}

(6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m)\right].

We prove that by induction on the size of
\mathcal{S}
.

Base case
|\mathcal{S}|=1
: :

\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
is trivial.

Base case
|\mathcal{S}|=2
: :

\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
is straightforward from NA.

Inductive case: :

Assuming
\mu
satisfies
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
for any partition
with size less than
K
,
we want to show that
\mu
satisfies
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
for any partition
with size equals to
K
.

Say
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{K}\}
.
For any family of all monotone or all antitone functions

f_{i}:\mathbf{Mem}[S_{i}]\to\mathcal{R}^{+}
,
either both
m\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{K-1}f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m)
and
f_{K}
are monotone,
or both
m\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{K-1}f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m)
and
f_{K}
are antitone.
Thus, by the inductive hypothesis

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{K}f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{K-1}f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m)\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[f_{K}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{K}}m)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=1}^{K-1}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m)\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[f_{K}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{K}}m)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m)\right].

The backward direction of the first equivalence is more involved.
For any two disjoint
A,B\subseteq S
, we know
\mu
satisfies
\{A,B\}
-
PNA
,
so for every pair of both monotone or both antitone functions

f:\mathbf{Mem}[A]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

g:\mathbf{Mem}[B]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)].

But the problem is to show this inequality when
f,g
are not both
non-negative. We prove that in three steps:

(1)

If
f,g
are lower-bounded by
-L
, i.e.,
f(x)\geq-L

and
g(x)\geq-L
for any
x
. Then
x\to f(x)+L
and

x\to g(x)+L
are both non-negative functions.
Thus,

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[(f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)+L)\cdot(g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)+L)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)+L]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)+L].

Meanwhile,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)+L)\cdot(g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)+L)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]+L\cdot\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]+L\cdot\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]+L^{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)+L]\cdot\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)+L]

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]+L)\cdot(\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]+L)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]+L\cdot\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]+L\cdot\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]+L^{2}.

So
Equation

7
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)].

(2)

If the codomain of
f
or
g
does not range across both negative and positive numbers,
then we can also prove the desired inequality by applying the monotone convergence theorem
on the result for lower-bounded functions.

•

Say
f
is non-negative and
g
is non-positive.
For any natural number
n
,
m\in\mathbf{Mem}[A\cup B]
,
we define
g_{n}(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)=\max(g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m),-n)
,

h_{n}(m)=f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g_{n}(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)
.
Then for any
n
,
g_{n}
and
h_{n}
are lower-bounded non-positive functions;
and for any
m
,
\{g_{n}(m)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a monotonically decreasing sequence
converging to
g(m)
,

\{h_{n}(m)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a monotonically decreasing sequence
converging to
f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)
.
By the monotone convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}h_{n}(m)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}g_{n}(\pi_{B}m).

By what we proved above, for any
n
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[h_{n}(m)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g_{n}(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]

Taking that to the limit
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[h_{n}(m)]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]

Therefore, for any distribution
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[A\cup B])
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)].

•

The case where
f
is non-positive and
g
is non-negative is symmetric.

•

The case where
f
and
g
are both non-positive is also similar.
We will define
f_{n}(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)=\max(f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m),-n)
,
g_{n}(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)=\max(g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m),-n)
,

h_{n}(m)=f_{n}(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g_{n}(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}h_{n}(m)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}g_{n}(\pi_{B}m)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}f(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}f_{n}(\pi_{A}m).

And the rest follows.

(3)

Now we consider the general case where we only know both
f
and
g

are either lower-bounded or upper bounded.

•

If both
f
and
g
are lower-bounded, reduce to the first case.

•

If
f
is lower-bounded by
L
,
g
is upper-bounded by
U
,
then we can consider function
f^{\prime}=f+L
and
g^{\prime}=g-U
.
Then
f^{\prime}
is non-negative and
g^{\prime}
is non-positive, so by step 2, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f^{\prime}(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g^{\prime}(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f^{\prime}(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g^{\prime}(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)].

By calculations analogous to what we did in step 1, that implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)].

•

If
f
is upper-bounded and
g
is lower-bounded: analogous to above.

•

If both
f
and
g
are upper-bounded: also, analogous to above.

Thus,
\mu
satisfies
\{A,B\}
-
PNA
implies
\mu
satisfies
(A,B)
-NA.
And therefore,
\mu
satisfies
\{A,B\}
-
PNA
for any
A,B\subseteq S

implies
S
satisfies strong NA in
\mu
.

∎

C.2.
A
\mathbf{2}
-BI model for independence and negative association

The proof that independence implies
PNA
will use the following lemma.

Lemma C.2.

In a distribution
\mu
, if
\mu
satisfies
\{S_{1},S_{2}\}
-
PNA
,

\mu
satisfies
\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
-
PNA
,
and
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
is independent from
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
in
\mu

then
\mu
is
\{S_{1}\cup T_{1},S_{2}\cup T_{2}\}
-
PNA
.

Proof.

By the definition of
PNA
and independence,
S_{1},S_{2}
are disjoint,

T_{1},T_{2}
are disjoint, and
S_{1}\cup T_{1},S_{2}\cup T_{2}
are disjoint.
For any monotonically decreasing/increasing functions

f:\mathbf{Mem}[S_{1}\cup T_{1}]\to\mathbb{R}^{+},g:\mathbf{Mem}[S_{2}\cup T_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{S_{1}\cup T_{1}}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{S_{2}\cup T_{2}}m)]

(By independence of
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
and
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu}\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\pi_{T_{1}\cup T_{2}}\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{S_{1}}s,\mathbf{p}_{T_{1}}t)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{S_{2}}s,\mathbf{p}_{T_{2}}t)]

(
\diamondsuit
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu}\left(\mathbb{E}_{t_{1}\sim\pi_{T_{1}}\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{S_{1}}s,t_{1})]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{t_{2}\sim\pi_{T_{2}}\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{S_{2}}s,t_{2})]\right)

(
\heartsuit
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s_{1}\sim\pi_{S_{1}}\mu}\mathbb{E}_{t_{1}\sim\pi_{T_{1}}\mu}[f(s_{1},t_{1})]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{s_{2}\sim\pi_{S_{2}}\mu}\mathbb{E}_{t_{2}\sim\pi_{T_{2}}\mu}[g(s_{2},t_{2})]

(
\clubsuit
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{S_{1}\cup T_{1}}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{S_{2}\cup T_{2}}m)]

where
\diamondsuit
is because
\pi_{T_{1}\cup T_{2}}\mu
is
T_{1},T_{2}
-
PNA
and
f(\mathbf{p}_{S_{1}}s,t_{1}),g(\mathbf{p}_{S_{2}}s,t_{2})
are both monotonically
decreasing/increasing in
T_{1},T_{2}
;
\heartsuit
is because
\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu
is
S_{1},S_{2}
-
PNA
and that
\mathbb{E}_{t_{1}\sim\pi_{T_{1}}\mu}[f(\mathbf{p}_{S_{1}}s,t_{1})]
, and
\mathbb{E}_{t_{2}\sim\pi_{T_{2}}\mu}[g(\mathbf{p}_{S_{2}}s,t_{2})]
are both monotonically decreasing/increasing in
S_{1},S_{2}
;
\clubsuit
is by independence of
S_{1}
and
T_{1}
and the independence of

S_{2}
and
T_{2}
in
\mu
.
∎

See
4.12

Proof.

Fix
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
.
Say
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{p}\}
and
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{q}\}
. For any

\mathcal{R}
coarsening
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
, indexing
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
as
\{U_{1},\dots,U_{p+q}\}
, indexing
\mathcal{R}
as

\{R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\{\cup\{U_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}\mid i\in[n]\}.

Then, given a family of monotonically increasing/decreasing functions

g_{i}:R_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}\left[\prod_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}\left[\prod_{i\in[n]}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{\cup\{U_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}}m)\right].

For each
i
,
\cup\{U_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}
can be divided into the part in
S
and the part in
T
. We refer to them as

S^{\prime}_{i}
and
T^{\prime}_{i}
. (Some of
S^{\prime}_{i}
and
T^{\prime}_{i}
may be empty).
Thus, for each
i
,

\displaystyle g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{\cup\{U_{j}\mid j\in f(i)\}}m)

\displaystyle=g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{i}}m).

By
Lemma

A.4
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{S^{\prime}_{1},\dots,S^{\prime}_{n}\}

coarsens
\mathcal{S}
, and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\{T^{\prime}_{1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{n}\}

coarsens
\mathcal{T}
. So
\mu
is
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
-
PNA
and

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
-
PNA
.

We prove by induction on
k\in[n]
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{i}}m)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}\left[g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{i}}m)\right].

Base case:

When
k=1
, trivial.

Inductive case:

For
k<n
, assume

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[\prod_{i\in[k]}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{i}}m)]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{i}}m)].

Note that
\mu
is
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
-
PNA
implies that

\mu
is
\{\cup_{i\in[k]}(S^{\prime}_{i}),S^{\prime}_{k+1}\}
-
PNA
, and

\mu
is
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
-
PNA
implies that
\{\cup_{i\in[k]}(T^{\prime}_{i}),T^{\prime}_{k+1}\}
-NA.
Thus, by
Lemma

C.2
,
\mu
is also

\{\{\cup_{i\in[k]}(S^{\prime}_{i})\cup\{\cup_{i\in[k]}(T^{\prime}_{i}),S^{\prime}_{k+1}\cup T^{\prime}_{k+1}\}
-NA.
Also, since all
g_{i}
is monotonically increasing (decreasing) and non-negative,

m\mapsto\prod_{i\in[k]}g_{i}(m)
is also a monotonically increasing (decreasing) function from
\cup_{i\in[k]}S^{\prime}_{i}\cup\cup_{i\in[k]}T^{\prime}_{i}
to
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[\prod_{i\in[k+1]}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{i}}m)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[\prod_{i\in[k]}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{i}}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g_{k+1}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{k+1}\cup T^{\prime}_{k+1}}m)]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in[k+1]}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{i}}m)],

where the second inequality follows from the inductive hypothesis.

Thus, the desired inequality holds for any
\mathcal{R}
coarsening
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
and any family of monotonically increasing (decreasing) functions on
\mathcal{R}
.
Thus,
\mu
is
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
.
∎

See
4.15

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{i,m}=(X_{i},\sqsubseteq_{i},\oplus_{i,m},E_{i,m})
.
For any
i\in\{1,2\}
,
m\in M
,
\mathcal{X}_{i,m}
is a BI frame.

First, for any
m\in M
,
we prove that
(X,\sqsubseteq,\oplus_{m},E_{m})
is a BI frame.

Down-Closed.:

Let
(z_{1},z_{2})\in(x_{1},x_{2})\oplus_{m}(y_{1},y_{2})
with

(x_{1},x_{2})\sqsupseteq(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})
and
(y_{1},y_{2})\sqsupseteq(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime})
.
Then, from the Down-Closed property of
\mathcal{X}_{1,m}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2,m}
respectively,
we have that there exists
z_{1}^{\prime}
and
z_{2}^{\prime}
such that

z_{i}\sqsupseteq_{i}z_{i}^{\prime}
and
z_{i}^{\prime}\in x_{i}^{\prime}\oplus y_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Hence
(z_{1},z_{2})\sqsupseteq(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime})
and
(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime})\in(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\oplus_{m}(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime})
.

Commutativity.:

Immediate.

Associativity.:

Let
(w_{1},w_{2})\in(t_{1},t_{2})\oplus_{m}(z_{1},z_{2})
and
(t_{1},t_{2})\in(x_{1},x_{2})\oplus_{m}(y_{1},y_{2})
.
Then for
i\in\{1,2\}
there exists
s_{i}\in y_{i}\oplus_{i,m}z_{i}
such that
w_{i}\in x_{i}\oplus_{i,m}s_{i}
.
Thus,
(s_{1},s_{2})\in(y_{1},y_{2})\oplus_{m}(z_{1},z_{2})
and
(w_{1},w_{2})\in(x_{1},x_{2})\oplus_{m}(s_{1},s_{2})
.

Unit Existence.:

Immediate.

Unit Closure.:

If
(e_{1},e_{2})\in E_{m}
and
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})\sqsupseteq(e_{1},e_{2})
, then
e_{i}^{\prime}\in E_{i,m}
, so

(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})\in E_{m}
.

Unit Coherence.:

Let
(e_{1},e_{2})\in E_{m}
and
(x_{1},x_{2})\in(y_{1},y_{2})\oplus_{m}(e_{1},e_{2})
. Then

x_{i}\sqsupseteq_{i}y_{i}
, which implies that
(x_{1},x_{2})\sqsupseteq(y_{1},y_{2})
.
∎

Second, we show
if
m\leq m^{\prime}\in M
, then
(\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{\prime})\oplus_{m}(\mu_{2},\mu_{2}^{\prime})\subseteq(\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{\prime})\oplus_{m^{\prime}}(\mu_{2},\mu_{2}^{\prime})
:

\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
are
M
-BI frames, so
m\leq m^{\prime}
implies that
\mu_{1}\oplus_{1,m}\mu_{2}\subseteq_{1}\mu_{1}\oplus_{1,m^{\prime}}\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{1}^{\prime}\oplus_{2,m^{\prime}}\mu_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq_{2}\mu_{1}^{\prime}\oplus_{2,m^{\prime}}\mu_{2}^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
(\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{\prime})\oplus_{m}(\mu_{2},\mu_{2}^{\prime})\subseteq(\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{\prime})\oplus_{m^{\prime}}(\mu_{2},\mu_{2}^{\prime})
.

Appendix D
Program Logic

D.1.
The semantics of
pWhile

Definition D.1 (Semantics of expressions).

We assume all expressions are well-typed.
We interpret them as
\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}]\times\mathbf{Mem}[T]\to\mathbf{Val}
, which can be naturally lifted to
\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}]\times\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[T])\to\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Val})
. For
\sigma\in\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}]
,
m\in\mathbf{Mem}[T]
,

\displaystyle\llbracket x_{d}\rrbracket(\sigma,m)

\displaystyle=\sigma(x_{d})

\displaystyle\llbracket x_{r}\rrbracket(\sigma,m)

\displaystyle=m(x_{r})

\displaystyle\llbracket[e_{1},\dots,e_{n}]\rrbracket(\sigma,m)

\displaystyle=[\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\sigma,m),\dots,\llbracket e_{n}\rrbracket(\sigma,m)]

\displaystyle\llbracket e+f\rrbracket(\sigma,m)

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket(\sigma,m)+\llbracket f\rrbracket(\sigma,m)

when
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\sigma,m)
and
\llbracket f_{r}\rrbracket(\sigma,m)
are numbers

\displaystyle\llbracket e\land f\rrbracket(\sigma,m)

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket(\sigma,m)\land\llbracket f\rrbracket(\sigma,m)

when
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\sigma,m)
and
\llbracket f_{r}\rrbracket(\sigma,m)
are booleans

Definition D.2 (Convex combination of distributions).

Let the binary operator
\circ_{\rho}
takes a convex combination of two distributions, i.e.,
for any
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[S])
, for any
x\in\mathbf{Mem}[S]
,

\displaystyle\mu_{1}\circ_{\rho}\mu_{2}(x)\triangleq\rho\cdot\mu_{1}(x)+(1-\rho)\cdot\mu_{2}(x).

Definition D.3 (Semantics of
pWhile
).

Let
\text{Unif}_{S}
denotes the uniform distribution on finite set
S
, i.e.,
\mu:s\mapsto\frac{1}{|S|}
for any
s\in S
.

We also assume that for any
\sigma\in\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}]
, any
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[T_{1}]),\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[T_{2}])
where
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{RV}
, the first component of

\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu_{1})
and
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu_{2})
are the same.
For any
(\sigma,\mu)\in\textsf{Config}
, let

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{skip}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)

\displaystyle\triangleq(\sigma,\mu)

\displaystyle\llbracket{x_{d}}\leftarrow{e_{d}}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)

\displaystyle\triangleq(\sigma[x_{d}\mapsto\llbracket e_{d}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)],\mu)

\displaystyle\llbracket{x_{r}}\leftarrow{e_{r}}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)

\displaystyle\triangleq(\sigma,\mathbf{bind}(\mu,m\mapsto\mathbf{unit}(m[x_{r}\mapsto\llbracket e_{r}\rrbracket(\sigma,m)])))

\displaystyle\llbracket{x_{r}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-0.75346pt}[0.75346pt]{\tiny$\mathdollar$}}}{{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}[0.86108pt]{$\leftarrow$}}}{\mathbf{U}_{S}}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)
