\displaystyle\triangleq(\sigma,\mathbf{bind}(\mu,m\mapsto\mathbf{bind}(\text{Unif}_{S},v\mapsto\mathbf{unit}(m[x_{r}\mapsto v]))))

\displaystyle\llbracket{c}\mathrel{;}{c^{\prime}}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)

\displaystyle\triangleq\llbracket c^{\prime}\rrbracket(\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu))

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{if}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c\ \mathbf{else}\ c^{\prime}(\sigma,\mu)\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)&\text{ if }\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\delta(\mathit{tt})\\
\llbracket c^{\prime}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)&\text{ if }\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\delta(\mathit{ff})\\
(\sigma,\mu_{1}\circ_{\rho}\mu_{2})&\text{otherwise, where }(\sigma,\mu_{1})=\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu\mid\llbracket b\rrbracket\sigma=\mathit{tt}),\\
\qquad&\rho=\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket\sigma=\mathit{tt}),\text{ and }(\sigma,\mu_{2})=\llbracket c^{\prime}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu\mid\llbracket b\rrbracket\sigma=\mathit{ff}).\end{cases}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{while}\ b\ \mathbf{do}\ c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)

\displaystyle\triangleq\lim_{n\to\infty}\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{n};\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ \mathbf{abort}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)

In the last case of the conditional, we write
\llbracket b\rrbracket\sigma
for the partial
evaluation of
b
on the deterministic memory and think of it as another
expression. So
\mu\mid\llbracket b\rrbracket\sigma=\mathit{tt}
and
\mu\mid\llbracket b\rrbracket\sigma=\mathit{ff}
are both conditional distribution. We assume that there is no update
to the deterministic memory when branching on randomized expressions, so the
deterministic memory of both
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu\mid\llbracket b\rrbracket\sigma=\mathit{tt})

and
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu\mid\llbracket b\rrbracket\sigma=\mathit{ff})
remains to be
\sigma
.

In the semantics for the while loop, we can see
\mathbf{abort}
as a command
that programmers does not have access to: for any
\sigma,\mu
,

\llbracket\mathbf{abort}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)
returns the zero sub-distribution. The
limit, taken with the point-wise order, exists because the sub-distribution’s
mass monotonically increases as
n
increases and is upper bounded by 1. In
practice, because we assumed that all loops terminates in finite steps, the
limit is always a full distribution, so all commands in
pWhile
can still be
interpreted as transformers from configurations to configurations.

D.2.
The atomic propositions and axioms

Definition D.4.

For any operation
\odot\in\{\land,\lor,\mathrel{\circledast},\mathrel{\ast}\}
, we use the
corresponding big-operation
\bigodot\in\left\{\bigwedge,\bigvee,\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}},\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}\right\}
.

•

For any constant or logical variable
N\geq 1
, let
\bigodot_{i=0}^{N}P_{i}=P_{0}
abbreviate
((P_{0}\odot P_{1})\odot\cdots)\odot P_{N-1}
. Formally, let
\bigodot_{i=0}^{N}P_{i}=P_{0}
if
N=1
, and
let
\bigodot_{i=0}^{N}P_{i}\triangleq\left(\bigodot_{i=0}^{N-1}P_{i}\right)\odot P_{N-1}
for
N>1
.

•

For a finite multi- set of formula
\{P_{i}\}_{i\in S}
, let
\bigodot_{s\in S}P_{s}
abbreviate
((P_{s_{0}}\odot P_{s_{1}})\odot\cdots)\odot P_{s_{k}}
,
where
s_{0},\dots,s_{k}
is an arbitrary ordering of
S
. The satisfaction
is not ambiguous since
\odot
is associative and commutative.

•

For any program variable
v\in\mathcal{DV}\cup\mathcal{RV}
, for any state

(\sigma,\mu)\models{v}\sim{N}
, we want
\bigodot_{i=0}^{v}P_{i}
to be equivalent to

\bigodot_{i=0}^{N}P_{i}
. Formally,
\bigodot_{i=0}^{v}P_{i}

abbreviates
\bigvee_{N\in\mathbf{Val}}({v}\sim{N}\land\bigodot_{i=0}^{N}P_{i})
.

See
5.3

Proof.

Forward direction: we fix
\sigma\in\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}]
.
Let
Y_{j}=\{y_{i}\mid 0\leq i\leq j\}

we prove by induction on
j
that

(\sigma,\pi_{Y_{j}}\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{i=0}^{j+1}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.

If
j=0
: then
y_{1}\in\mathbf{dom}(\mu)
, and
(\sigma,\pi_{\{y_{1}\}}\mu)\models\langle y_{1}\rangle
.

If
j\geq 1
: Assuming
(\sigma,\pi_{Y_{j-1}}\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{i=0}^{j1}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.
Since
Y
satisfies NA in
\mu
, by
Theorem

4.7
,
\mu
is
\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
for any partition
\mathcal{T}
of
Y
.
In particular, for any partition
\mathcal{T}_{1}
on
Y_{j-1}
and any partition
\mathcal{T}_{2}
on
\{y_{j}\}
,

\mu
must be
\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{2}
-
PNA
.
Thus,

\pi_{Y_{j}}\mu\in\pi_{Y_{j-1}}\mu\oplus\pi_{\{y_{j}\}}\mu
.
Since
(\sigma,\pi_{Y_{j-1}}\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{i=0}^{j}\langle y_{i}\rangle

and
(\sigma,\pi_{\{y_{j}\}}\mu)\models\langle y_{j}\rangle
,
that implies
(\sigma,\pi_{Y_{j}}\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{i=0}^{j+1}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.

Thus,
(\sigma,\pi_{Y_{j}}\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{i=0}^{j+1}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.
Take
j=K
, we have
(\sigma,\pi_{Y}\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{i=0}^{K}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.
By persistence,
(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{i=0}^{K}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.

Backward direction: for any
A,B
being disjoint subsets of
Y
,
by commutativity and associativity of
\mathrel{\circledast}
, we can
reorder formula and get

(\sigma,\mu)\models\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{y_{i}\in A}\langle y_{i}\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{y_{i}\in B}\langle y_{i}\rangle\right)\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{y_{i}\in(T\setminus(A\cup B))}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.
By satisfaction rules and the definition of
\oplus
, there exists
\mu^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mu
such that

(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{y_{i}\in A}\langle y_{i}\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{y_{i}\in B}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.
By satisfaction rules again,
there exists
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu^{\prime\prime}
such that

\mu^{\prime}\sqsupseteq\mu^{\prime\prime}\in\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}
,
and
(\sigma,\mu_{1})\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{y_{i}\in A}\langle y_{i}\rangle
,
and
(\sigma,\mu_{2})\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{y_{i}\in B}\langle y_{i}\rangle
.
Note that
\mu_{1}
is trivially
\{A\}
-
PNA
,
and
\mu_{2}
is trivially
\{B\}
-
PNA
.
Thus,
\mu^{\prime\prime}
satisfies
\{A,B\}
-
PNA
.

Therefore,
\mu
satisfies
(A,B)
-NA for any
A,B
being disjoint subsets of
Y
,
i.e.,
\mu
satisfies NA on
Y
.
∎

See
5.4

Proof.

For any state
(\sigma,\mu)
satisfying
\mathbf{OH}_{N}\langle[x_{1},\dots,x_{N-1}]\rangle
,
by
Theorem

2.2
,

\{x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\}
satisfies NA in
\mu
,
and by
Theorem

5.3
,

(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=1}^{N}\langle x_{\gamma}\rangle
.
Similarly,
by
Theorem

2.2
,

\{x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\}
satisfies NA in
\mu
,
and by
Theorem

5.3
,

(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=1}^{N}\langle x_{\gamma}\rangle
.

∎

Lemma D.5.

Given a distribution
\mu
with domain
S
.
Let
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{N}\}
be a partition of
S
,

\{f_{i}:\mathbf{Mem}[S_{i}]\to\mathbf{Mem}[T_{i}]\}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}
be a family of non-decreasing functions (or a family of non-increasing functions),
and
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{N}\}
be a partition of another set
T
.
Let

\mu^{\prime}=\mathbf{bind}\Big(\mu,m\mapsto\bigoplus_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{unit}(f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m))\Big).

If
\mu
satisfies
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
, then
\mu^{\prime}
satisfies

\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for any
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{1},\dots,R_{m}\}
that coarsens
\mathcal{T}
,
for any family of non-negative non-increasing (or non-decreasing) functions
\{g_{i}:\mathbf{Mem}[R_{i}]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\}_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}
,

\mathbb{E}_{m^{\prime}\sim\mu^{\prime}}\left[\prod_{i}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime})\right]\leq\prod_{i}\mathbb{E}_{m^{\prime}\sim\mu^{\prime}}\left[g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime})\right].

Our first step is to show that if we can obtain a distribution on
\mathcal{R}
by first applying monotone map and then coarsening, then we can also obtaining that by first coarsening and then applying monotone map:
For any
\mathcal{R}
that coarsens
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{n}\}
, there exists some coarsening function
g
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\{\cup\{T_{j}\mid j\in g(i)\}\mid i\in[n]\}.

Let
R_{i}=\cup\{T_{j}\mid j\in g(i)\}
.
Since
g
is a coarsening function,
for each
i\in[n]
,
there exists exactly one
k_{i}
(i.e., the index for the component in the coarsened function) such that
g(k_{i})\ni i
.
Then,

T_{i}\subseteq\cup\{T_{j}\mid j\in g(k_{i})\}=R_{k_{i}}
.

Let
R^{\prime}_{i}=\cup\{S_{j}\mid j\in g(i)\}
,
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\{R^{\prime}_{i}\mid i\in[n]\}
.
Then
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
coarsens
\mathcal{S}
.
Define
h_{i}:\mathbf{Mem}[R^{\prime}_{i}]\to\mathbf{Mem}[R_{i}]
by having

h_{i}(m)=\bowtie_{j\in g(i)}f_{j}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{j}}m)
.
Since each
f_{i}
is monotone, then each
h_{i}
also monotone in the point-wise order. Then,

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}=\mathbf{bind}\Big(\mu,m\mapsto\bigoplus_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{unit}(f_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{i}}m))\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}\Big(\mu,m\mapsto\bigoplus_{R^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\mathbf{unit}(h_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m))\Big),

so
\mu^{\prime}
is equivalent to applying
h_{i}
on each component of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Also, since
\mu
is
\mathcal{S}
-
PNA
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
coarsens
\mathcal{S}
,
for any family of non-negative non-increasing (or non-decreasing) functions
\{g_{i}:\mathbf{Mem}[R^{\prime}_{i}]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\}_{R^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
,

(8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}\left[\prod_{i}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m)\right]\leq\prod_{i}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m).

Our second step is to show that this inequality is preserved under monotone maps.

•

If every
h_{i}
is non-increasing and
\{g_{i}:\mathbf{Mem}[R_{i}]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\}_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}
are non-decreasing,
is non-increasing,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m^{\prime}\sim\mu^{\prime}}[\prod_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Mem}[T]}\mu^{\prime}(m^{\prime})\cdot\prod_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Mem}[T]}\sum_{m\in\mathbf{Mem}[S]}\mu(m)\cdot(\prod_{R^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\mathbf{unit}(h_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m))(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime}))\cdot\prod_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbf{Mem}[S]}\mu(m)\cdot\prod_{R^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}g_{i}(h_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[\prod_{R^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}g_{i}(h_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m))].

Note that
g_{i}\circ f
is non-negative non-decreasing,
then since
\mu
satisfies
Equation

8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[\prod_{R^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}g_{i}(h_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m))]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{R^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu}[g_{i}(h_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m))]

\displaystyle=\prod_{R^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\sum_{m\in\mathbf{Mem}[S]}\mu(m)\cdot g_{i}(h_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R^{\prime}_{i}}m))

\displaystyle=\prod_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}\mathbb{E}_{m^{\prime}\sim\mu^{\prime}}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime}).

Combined, we have
\mathbb{E}_{m^{\prime}\sim\mu^{\prime}}[\prod_{i}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime})]\leq\prod_{i}\mathbb{E}_{m^{\prime}\sim\mu^{\prime}}g_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{R_{i}}m^{\prime})
.

•

When
f_{i}
is non-increasing and
\{g_{i}\}
are non-negative non-decreasing,
or when
f_{i}
is non-decreasing and
\{g_{i}\}
are non-negative non-decreasing/non-increasing,
the proof is analogous.

∎

See
5.5

Proof.

Abbreviate
\{\bigcup_{\alpha=0}^{K_{\gamma}+1}\{x_{\gamma,\alpha}\}\mid 1\leq\gamma<M\}
as
X_{M}
.

For any state
(\sigma,\mu)
satisfying
\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{N}(\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{K_{\gamma}+1}\langle x_{\gamma,\alpha}\rangle)
,
we show by induction on
M
that: for
1\leq M\leq N
,

•

(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{M}(\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{K_{\gamma}}\langle x_{\gamma,\alpha}\rangle)
;

•

And
\mu
satisfies
X_{M}
-
PNA
implies

\mu
satisfies
X_{N}
-
PNA
.

Assuming that it is true for
M
, we show that for
M-1
.
By assumption,
(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{M}(\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{K_{\gamma}+1}\langle x_{\gamma,\alpha}\rangle)
,
so there exists
\mu^{\prime},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
such that
\mu\sqsupseteq\mu^{\prime}\in\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}
,

(\sigma,\mu_{1})\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{M-1}(\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{K_{\gamma}+1}\langle x_{\gamma,\alpha}\rangle)
and
(\sigma,\mu_{2})\models\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{K_{M}+1}\langle x_{M,\alpha}\rangle
. Thus,
\mu^{\prime}
is
X_{M}
-
PNA
if
\mu_{1}
is
X_{M-1}
-
PNA
and
\mu_{2}
is
\cup_{\alpha=0}^{K_{M}+1}\{x_{M,\alpha}\}
-
PNA
. Thus,

•

By the definition of
\oplus
, we have
\mu\sqsupseteq\mu_{1}
,
and by persistence
(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{M-1}(\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{K_{\gamma}+1}\langle x_{\gamma,\alpha}\rangle)
;

•

Since
(\sigma,\mu_{1})\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=0}^{M-1}(\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{K_{\gamma}+1}\langle x_{\gamma,\alpha}\rangle)
, we have
\cup X_{M-1}
is inside
\mathbf{dom}(\sigma)\cup\mathbf{dom}(\mu_{1})
. Thus,
if
\mu
satisfies
X_{M-1}
-
PNA
, then
\mu_{1}\sqsubseteq\mu
satisfies
X_{M-1}
-
PNA
.
Trivially,
\mu_{2}
is
\cup_{\alpha=0}^{K_{M}}\{x_{M,\alpha}\}
-
PNA
.
Thus,
\mu^{\prime}
is
X_{M}
-
PNA
, and
\mu
is
X_{M}
-
PNA
.
By inductive assumption,
\mu
is
X_{N}
-
PNA
.

Then, letting
M=1
would give us:
\mu
satisfies
X_{1}
-
PNA
implies

\mu
satisfies
X_{N}
-
PNA
.

X_{1}
is a partition of one component, so
\mu
satisfying
X_{1}
-
PNA
is trivial.
Therefore,
\mu
satisfies
X_{N}
-
PNA
.

Since
\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{N}y_{\gamma}=f_{\gamma}(x_{\gamma,1},\dots,x_{\gamma,K_{\gamma}})
and
f_{\gamma}
are all antitone or monotone,
by
Lemma

D.5
,

\mu
satisfies
\{\{y_{\gamma}\}\mid 1\leq\gamma<N\}
-
PNA
.
Then we can show
(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=1}^{N}\langle y_{\gamma}\rangle

through another simple induction or by applying the existing theorems.
If we do that by applying the theorems,

Theorem

4.7
implies that
if
\mu
satisfies
\{\{y_{\gamma}\}\mid 1\leq\gamma\leq N\}
-
PNA
,
then
\{\{y_{\gamma}\}\mid 1\leq\gamma\leq N\}
satisfies NA in
\mu
.
Then, by
Theorem

5.3
,
(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\gamma=1}^{N}\langle y_{\gamma}\rangle
.
∎

D.3.
The restriction property

We prove the restriction on deterministic memories and on randomized memories
by separate induction, and then combine them.

Lemma D.6 (Restriction on deterministic memories).

Let
(\sigma,\mu)
be any configuration in
Config
,
and let
\phi
be a
M
-BI
{}_{\text{restricted}}
formula interpreted on
\mathcal{X}_{\textsf{comb}}
,
Then, for any
m\in\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}\setminus\text{FV}(\phi)]
,

(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\iff(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu)\models\phi.

Proof.

Note that the two directions are symmetric, so we only prove the forward direction.

We prove it by induction on the syntax of formula.
Most cases are straightforward, so we only show three cases.

\phi=P\to Q
: :

Assuming
(\sigma,\mu)\models P\to Q
,
that says for any
(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\sqsupseteq(\sigma,\mu)
,
if
(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models P
, then
(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models Q
.

For any
(\widehat{\sigma},\widehat{\mu})\sqsupseteq(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu)
,
it must
\widehat{\sigma}=\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m
and
\widehat{\mu}\sqsupseteq\mu
.
If
(\widehat{\sigma},\widehat{\mu})\models P
,
then by
\widehat{\sigma}=\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m

and the inductive hypothesis,
(\sigma,\widehat{\mu})\models P
;
since
(\sigma,\mu)\models P\to Q
and
\widehat{\mu}\sqsupseteq\mu
,

(\sigma,\widehat{\mu})\models P
implies
(\sigma,\widehat{\mu})\models Q
;
and by inductive hypothesis again,
(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\widehat{mu})\models Q
,
Thus,
(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu)\models P\to Q
.

\phi=P\mathrel{\circledast}Q
: :

Assuming
(\sigma,\mu)\models P\mathrel{\circledast}Q
,
then there exists

(\widehat{\sigma},\widehat{\mu}),(\sigma_{1},\mu_{1}),(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2})
such that

(\sigma,\mu)\sqsupseteq(\widehat{\sigma},\widehat{\mu})\in(\sigma_{1},\mu_{1})\oplus(\sigma_{2},\mu_{2})
,

(\sigma_{1},\mu_{1})\models P
,
and
(\sigma_{2},\mu_{2})\models Q
.
By the definition of the pre-order and
\oplus
,
it must
\sigma=\widehat{\sigma}=\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
,

\mu\sqsupseteq\widehat{\mu}\in\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}
.

By inductive hypothesis,

(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma_{1}\bowtie m,\mu_{1})=(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu_{1})\models P
,
and
(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma_{2}\bowtie m,\mu_{2})=(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu_{2})\models Q
.
Also,

(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu)\sqsupseteq(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\widehat{\mu})\in(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu_{1})\oplus(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu_{2}).

So
(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu)\models P\mathrel{\circledast}Q
.

\phi=P\mathrel{\ast}Q
:

Analogous as the case for
P\mathrel{\circledast}Q
.

\phi=P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}Q
: :

Assuming
(\sigma,\mu)\models P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}Q
,
that says for any

(\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime})\in(\sigma,\mu)\otimes(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
,
if
(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models P
,
then
(\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime})\models Q
.

For any
(\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime})\in(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu)\otimes(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
,
it must
\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}=\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m
,
\mu^{\prime\prime}\in\mu\otimes\mu^{\prime}
.
Thus,
(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models P
is equivalent to
(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu^{\prime})\models P
, and by inductive hypothesis,
that is equivalent to
(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})\models P
.
It also follows that
(\sigma,\mu^{\prime\prime})\in(\sigma,\mu)\otimes(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})
.
Since
(\sigma,\mu)\models P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}Q
and
(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})\models P
,
we have
(\sigma,\mu^{\prime\prime})\models Q
.
By inductive hypothesis,
(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu^{\prime\prime})\models Q
,
and equivalently
(\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime})\models Q
.

Thus,
(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\mu)\models P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}Q
.

∎

Lemma D.7 (Restriction on randomized memories).

Let
(\sigma,\mu)
be any configuration in
Config
,
and let
\phi
be a
M
-BI
{}_{\text{restricted}}
formula interpreted on
\mathcal{X}_{\textsf{comb}}
,
Then,

(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\iff(\sigma,\pi_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\mu)\models\phi.

Proof.

The reverse direction follows by the Kripke monotonicity,
and the forward direction follows by induction on
\phi
.
The proof for most of the inductive cases is very similar to the proof that
the probabilistic model in
Barthe
et al. 2020
satisfies restriction.
The new inductive case is:

•

\phi\equiv P\mathrel{\circledast}Q
.
Then,
\mu\models\phi
iff there exists
\mu^{\prime},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
s.t.
\mu\sqsupseteq\mu^{\prime}\in\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}
,
\mu_{1}\models P
and
\mu_{2}\models Q
.
By induction,
\pi_{FV(P)}\mu_{1}\models P
and
\pi_{FV(Q)}\mu_{2}\models Q
.
Note that
\pi_{FV(P)}\mu_{1}\sqsubseteq\mu_{1}
and
\pi_{FV(Q)}\mu_{2}\sqsubseteq\mu_{2}
.
By Down-closure, there exists
\mu^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\mu^{\prime}
such that

\mu^{\prime\prime}\in\pi_{FV(P)}\mu_{1}\oplus\pi_{FV(Q)}\mu_{2}
.
This
\mu^{\prime\prime}
satisfies
P\mathrel{\circledast}Q
.
By definition of
\oplus
in the
PNA
model,

dom(\mu^{\prime\prime})=dom(\pi_{FV(P)}\mu_{1})\cup dom(\pi_{FV(Q)}\mu_{2})=FV(P)\cup FV(Q)=FV(P\mathrel{\circledast}Q).

Also, by the definition of the pre-order,
\mu^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\mu^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mu

implies that
\mu^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{dom(\mu^{\prime\prime})}\mu=\pi_{FV(\phi)}\mu
.
Thus,
\pi_{FV(\phi)}\mu=\mu^{\prime\prime}\models\phi
.

∎

See
5.8

Proof.

Based on
Lemma

D.6
and
Lemma

D.7
,

(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\iff(\sigma,\pi_{\phi}\mu)\models\phi\iff(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\sigma\bowtie m,\pi_{\text{FV}(\phi)}\mu)\models\phi.

∎

For the counterexample of the restriction property, we prove a lemma.

Lemma D.8.

Let
\sigma\in\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}]
be “empty” – let every deterministic variable be undefined,

\mu
be the uniform distribution over one hot vectors on
A,B
,
and
\phi=(\mathbf{U}_{\{0,1\}}\langle C\rangle)\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu\circledast}(\langle B\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\langle C\rangle)
.
Then,
(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi
.

Proof.

Fix any
\mu_{C}
such that
(\sigma,\mu_{C})\models\mathbf{U}_{\{0,1\}}\langle C\rangle
,
which implies that
\pi_{C}\mu_{C}(0)=0.5
and
\pi_{C}\mu_{C}(1)=0.5
.
Fix
\mu_{e}\in\mu\oplus\mu_{C}
.

Since
B\in\mathbf{dom}(\mu)
,
\mu
is trivially
\{\{B\}\}
-
PNA
.
Similarly,
\mu_{C}
is trivially
\{\{C\}\}
-
PNA
.
Thus,
\mu_{e}\in\mu\oplus\mu_{C}
must be
\{\{B\},\{C\}\}
-
PNA
.
Then for any two both monotone or antitone functions
f:\mathbf{Mem}[B]\to\mathbb{R}^{+},g:\mathbf{Mem}[C]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[f(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{C}m)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[f(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[g(\mathbf{p}_{C}m)].

Similarly,
\mu_{e}\in\mu\oplus\mu_{C}
must be
\{\{A\},\{C\}\}
-
PNA
,
and for any two both monotone or antitone functions
f:\mathbf{Mem}[A]\to\mathbb{R}^{+},g:\mathbf{Mem}[C]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{C}m)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[g(\mathbf{p}_{C}m)].

Suppose variables
B
and
C
are not independent in
\mu_{e}
,
then by
Lemma

A.1
,
there must exists some both monotone or both antitone functions
f:\mathbf{Mem}[B]\to\mathbb{R}^{+},g:\mathbf{Mem}[C]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[f(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{C}m)]<\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[f(\mathbf{p}_{B}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[g(\mathbf{p}_{C}m)]

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 0.5\cdot f(0)\cdot\left(g(1)\cdot P(C=1\mid B=0)+g(0)\cdot P(C=0\mid B=0)\right)

\displaystyle+0.5\cdot f(1)\cdot\left(g(1)\cdot P(C=1\mid B=1)+g(0)\cdot P(C=0,B=1)\right)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle(0.5\cdot f(0)+0.5\cdot f(1))\cdot(0.5\cdot g(1)+0.5\cdot g(0)),

where
P(\dots)
denotes the respective probability that in
\mu_{e}
.
Since
\mu_{e}\in\mu\oplus\mu_{C}
,
we have
\mu_{e}\sqsupseteq\mu
,
and
\mu
being a uniform distribution over one-hot vectors on
A,B
indicates that
for any
m
in the support of
\mu_{e}
,
A=1
iff
B=0
, and
A=0
iff
B=1
.
Therefore,

P(C=v\mid B=0)=P(C=v\mid A=1)
and
P(C=v\mid B=1)=P(C=v\mid A=0).

Also, by Bayes theorem, we have

\displaystyle P(C=v\mid A=0)\cdot P(A=0)+P(C=v\mid A=1)\cdot P(A=1)=P(C=v)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle P(C=v\mid A=0)\cdot 0.5+P(C=v\mid A=1)\cdot 0.5=0.5

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle P(C=v\mid A=0)=1-P(C=v\mid A=1).

Let
X=P(C=1\mid A=1)
,
Y=P(C=0\mid A=1)
, then we have

\displaystyle 0.5\cdot f(0)\cdot\left(g(1)\cdot X+g(0)\cdot Y\right)+0.5\cdot f(1)\cdot\left(g(1)\cdot(1-X)+g(0)\cdot(1-Y)\right)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle(0.5\cdot f(0)+0.5\cdot f(1))\cdot(0.5\cdot g(1)+0.5\cdot g(0))

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle f(0)\cdot g(1)\cdot(X-0.5)+f(0)\cdot g(0)\cdot(Y-0.5)+f(1)\cdot g(1)\cdot(0.5-X)+f(1)\cdot g(0)\cdot(0.5-Y)<0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle(f(0)-f(1))\cdot g(1)\cdot(X-0.5)+(f(0)-f(1))\cdot g(0)\cdot(Y-0.5)<0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 0.5\cdot f(1)\cdot\left(g(1)\cdot X+g(0)\cdot Y\right)+0.5\cdot f(0)\cdot\left(g(1)\cdot(1-X)+g(0)\cdot(1-Y)\right)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle(0.5\cdot f(0)+0.5\cdot f(1))\cdot(0.5\cdot g(1)+0.5\cdot g(0)).

Viewing
f
as a function from
\mathbf{Mem}[A]
to
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)\cdot g(\mathbf{p}_{C}m)]<\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[f(\mathbf{p}_{A}m)]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{m\sim\mu_{e}}[g(\mathbf{p}_{C}m)].

The last inequality contradicts
Equation

9
.

Therefore,
B
and
C
must be independent in
\mu_{e}
.
Hence,
\mu_{e}\models\langle B\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\langle C\rangle
, and
\mu\models\phi
.

∎

See
5.9

Proof.

Let
A,B,C
be three variables in
\mathcal{RV}
.
Let
\phi=(\mathbf{U}_{\{0,1\}}\langle C\rangle)\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu\circledast}(\langle B\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\langle C\rangle)
.
Let
\sigma
be a deterministic memory where every deterministic variable is undefined,
and
\mu
be the uniform distribution over one hot vectors on
A,B
.
Then, we claim
(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi

but
(\sigma,\pi_{\{B,C\}}\mu)\not\models\phi
.
For
(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi
, it suffices to show that for any
\mu_{C}

where
C
’s value is the uniform distribution on
\{0,1\}
, for any
\mu^{\prime}\sqsupseteq\mu
, and
\mu_{e}\in\mu^{\prime}\oplus\mu_{C}
,
B
and
C
are
independent in
\mu_{e}
according to
Lemma

D.8
.
The intuition is that
\mu_{e}
must satisfies
\{B,C\}
-
PNA
and
\{A,C\}
-
PNA
,
and since
A
’s value is always the opposite of
B
’s value,

(B,C)
has to satisfy pairwise independence in
\mu_{e}
.
To show
(\sigma,\pi_{\{B,C\}}\mu)\not\models\phi
,
we first note that
\pi_{\{B,C\}}\mu=\pi_{\{B\}}\mu
is a uniform distribution of
0
and
1
on
B
.
Let
\mu_{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[\{C\}])
be the uniform distribution on
\{0,1\}
,

\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[\{B,C\}])
be the uniform distribution over one-hot vectors on
B,C
.
Clearly,
B,C
are not independent in
\mu^{\prime}
, so
\mu^{\prime}\not\models\langle B\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\langle C\rangle
.
Also,
\mu^{\prime}
is in
\pi_{\{B,C\}}\mu\oplus\mu_{C}^{\prime}
. So
\pi_{B,C}\mu\not\models\mathbf{U}_{\{0,1\}}\langle C\rangle\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu\circledast}(\langle A\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\langle C\rangle)
.
∎

D.4.
The proof system of the program logic

The proof for the soundness of the frame rule relies on the following corollary of

Lemma

D.5
.

Lemma D.9 (Monotone map closure
PNA
(specific case)).

Let
S,T\subseteq\mathbf{Var}
be two disjoint sets of variables, and
\mathcal{T}

is sub-partition of
T
. Suppose that
f:\mathbf{Mem}[S]\to\mathbf{Mem}[S^{\prime}]
is a
monotonically non-decreasing non-negative function, and
S^{\prime}
disjoint from

T
. If
\mu
with domain
S\cup T
satisfies
\{S\}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
, then
\mathbf{bind}(\mu,m\mapsto\mathbf{unit}(f(\pi_{S}m))\oplus\mathbf{unit}(\pi_{T}m))\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[S^{\prime}\cup T])
satisfies
\{S^{\prime}\}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
.

Proof.

We can reduce to
Lemma

D.5
:
Let
S_{1}=S
,
T_{1}=S^{\prime}
,
and
\{S_{2},\dots,S_{n}\}=\{T_{2},\dots,T_{n}\}=\mathcal{T}
.
Let
f_{1}=f
and the rest of
f_{i}
to be the identity map.
Note that we have assumed to only work with non-negative values, so identity maps are also monotonically non-decreasing non-negative function.
∎

See
5.11

Proof.

We only prove the cases not already in
Barthe
et al. 2020
.

Rule:
Cond
.:

For any configuration
(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi
,
by side-condition that
\models\phi\rightarrow\mathbf{Detm}\langle b\rangle
,

(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathbf{Detm}\langle b\rangle
.
Thus,
\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)
must be a Dirac distribution.
Since the commands are well-typed,
\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)
is a distribution over booleans.
So
\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)
is either a Dirac distribution of truthful value
\mathit{tt}

or a Dirac distribution of false value
\mathit{ff}
.

If
\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)
is a Dirac distribution of truthful value
\mathit{tt}
,
then for any
m
in the support of
\mu
,
\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,m)=\mathit{tt}
,
and thus,
(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{tt}}
.
By the side-condition
\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{tt}}}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\psi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

and inductive hypothesis that this judgement is sound,

\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\psi
.
When
\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\delta(\mathit{tt})
, the semantics say that
\llbracket\mathbf{if}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c\ \mathbf{else}\ c^{\prime}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\psi
.

Symmetrically, when
\llbracket b\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\delta(\mathit{ff})
,

\llbracket\mathbf{if}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c\ \mathbf{else}\ c^{\prime}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\llbracket c^{\prime}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\psi
.

Rule:
Loop
.:

For any
(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi
, the side condition implies
(\sigma,\mu)\models\mathbf{Detm}\langle b\rangle
. We show by induction that for any
n
,

\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{n}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\land\mathbf{Detm}\langle b\rangle
,
and

\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{n}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{ff}}
implies

\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{n+1}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{ff}}
.

Say
(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})=\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{n}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)
. Assuming
(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models\phi\land\mathbf{Detm}\langle b\rangle
, there are two possibilities:

•:

(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{ff}}
, then

\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{n+1}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\llbracket\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c\rrbracket(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})=(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{ff}}.

•:

(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{tt}}
, then

\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{n+1}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\llbracket\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c\rrbracket(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})=\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models\phi,

where the last satisfaction is guaranteed by
\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{tt}}}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}
.
Since
\models\phi\to\mathbf{Detm}\langle b\rangle
, so
\llbracket\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c\rrbracket(\sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\models\phi\land\mathbf{Detm}\langle b\rangle
.

Since we assumed that the loop ends in finite step,
there exists a finite number
N
such that

\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{N}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{ff}}
and
also
\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{N-1}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{tt}}

if
N>1
.

Then

\llbracket\mathbf{while}\ b\ \mathbf{do}\ c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=\llbracket(\mathbf{if}_{R}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ c)^{N}\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\land{b}\sim{\mathit{ff}}
.

Rule:
RCase
.:

For any
(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi\mathrel{\ast}\eta
,
there exists
\mu^{\prime},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
such that
\mu\sqsupseteq\mu^{\prime}\in\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}

and
(\sigma,\mu_{1})\models\phi
,
(\sigma,\mu_{2})\models\eta
.

Say
\mu_{2}
is in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[T])
, then for any
m
in the support of
\mu_{2}
,
the conditional distribution
\mu_{2}\mid T=m
is a Dirac distribution of
m
,
i.e.,
\delta(m)
.
Since
\eta\in\text{CC}
(closed under conditioning),
so
(\sigma,\delta(m))\models\eta
,
and thus
(\sigma,\delta(m))\models\bigvee_{\alpha\in S}\eta_{\alpha}
.
Then, there exists
\alpha
such that
(\sigma,\delta(m))\models\eta_{\alpha}
.
Since
\mathbf{dom}(\mu_{1})
and
\mathbf{dom}(\mu_{2})
are independent in
\mu^{\prime}
,
the conditional distribution
\mu^{\prime}\mid T=m
is in
\mu_{1}\oplus\delta(m)
.
So
\mu^{\prime}\mid T=m\models\phi\mathrel{\ast}\eta_{\alpha}
.
By the side-condition
\vdash\phi\mathrel{\ast}\eta_{\alpha}
and inductive hypothesis that
it is sound, we have
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime}\mid T=m)\models\psi
.

For any command
c
, any condition
b
,
let
(\sigma_{b},\mu_{b})=\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime}\mid b)
and
(\sigma_{b},\mu_{\neg b})=\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime}\mid\neg b)
.
We can show by induction on the semantics of commands that

\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})
is a convex combination of
(\sigma,\mu^{\prime}\mid b)

and
(\sigma,\mu^{\prime}\mid\neg b)
:

\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})=(\sigma,(\mu^{\prime}\mid b)\circ_{\mu^{\prime}(b=\mathit{tt})}(\mu^{\prime}\mid\neg b))
.
Thus,
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})
is a convex combination of
all
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime}\mid T=m)
, where
m
in the support of
\mu^{\prime}
.
Since each of
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime}\mid T=m)
satisfies
\psi
and
\psi
is
closed under mixture,
we have
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})\models\psi
.
We can also show by induction on the semantics of commands that

\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\sqsupseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu^{\prime})
if
\mu\sqsupseteq\mu^{\prime}
.
So by persistence
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\psi
.

Rule:
ProbBound
.:

For any program state
(\sigma,\mu)\models\Pr[ev_{1}]\geq 1-\epsilon
,
let event
ev_{1}^{\sigma}:\mathbf{Mem}[\mathbf{dom}(\mu)]\to\{0,1\}
be the result of partially
interpreting
ev_{1}
on
\sigma
, i.e.,

ev_{1}^{\sigma}=\text{curry}(\llbracket ev_{1}\rrbracket)(\sigma)
,
and denote the function
\lambda x.1-ev_{1}^{\sigma}(x)
as
\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}
.
We also write
\Pr_{\mu}[ev_{1}^{\sigma}]
for

\sum_{m\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[\mathbf{dom}(\mu)])}\mu(m)\cdot ev_{1}^{\sigma}(m)
.

We can express
\mu
as the convex combination of two conditional distributions,
i.e.,

\mu=\Pr_{\mu}[ev_{1}^{\sigma}]\cdot(\mu\mid ev_{1}^{\sigma})+\Pr_{\mu}[\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}]\cdot(\mu\mid\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}).

Let
(\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{ev_{1}^{\sigma}})=\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,(\mu\mid ev_{1}^{\sigma}))
.
Since assignments to deterministic memories can only use variables in the
deterministic memories, there exists probabilistic memories
\mu_{\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}}

such that

(\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}})=\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,(\mu\mid\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}))
.
Then, by induction on the denotational semantics,
we can prove that

\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=(\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{ev_{1}^{\sigma}}\circ_{\Pr_{\mu}[ev_{1}^{\sigma}]}\mu_{\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}})
.

By construction,
\llbracket ev_{1}\rrbracket(\sigma,(\mu\mid ev_{1}^{\sigma}))=1
,
so
(\sigma,(\mu\mid ev_{1}^{\sigma}))\models ev_{1}
.
Also, by the assumption and inductive hypothesis, we have

\models\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{ev_{1}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[ev_{2}]\geq 1-\delta}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}
,
which implies

\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,(\mu\mid ev_{1}^{\sigma}))=(\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{ev_{1}^{\sigma}})\models\Pr[ev_{2}]\geq 1-\delta.

By definition, that means

\Pr_{(\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{ev_{1}^{\sigma}})}[ev_{2}]\geq 1-\delta
.
Then, by the law of total probability,

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{ev_{1}^{\sigma}}\circ_{\Pr_{\mu}[ev_{1}^{\sigma}]}\mu_{\neg ev})}[ev_{1}^{\sigma}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{(\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{ev_{1}^{\sigma}})}[ev_{2}]+\Pr_{\mu}[\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}]

(because
\Pr_{\mu}[\neg ev_{1}^{\sigma}]=\Pr_{\sigma,\mu}[\neg ev_{1}]
)

\displaystyle\leq\delta+\Pr_{\sigma,\mu}[\neg ev_{1}]

(because
(\sigma,\mu)\models\Pr[ev_{1}]\geq 1-\epsilon
,)

\displaystyle\leq\delta+\epsilon

Therefore,

\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)\models\Pr[ev_{2}]\leq\delta+\epsilon
.

Rule:
NegFrame
.:

For any
(\sigma,\mu)\models ev_{1}\mathrel{\ast}\eta
,
there exists
k_{1},k_{2},\mu^{\prime}
such that
\mu\sqsupseteq\mu^{\prime}\in k_{1}\oplus k_{2}
, and

(\sigma,k_{1})\models\phi
and
(\sigma,k_{2})\models\eta
.

Let
S_{1}\triangleq\mathbf{dom}(k_{1})
,
and note that
(\sigma,k_{1})\models\phi
and
\models\phi\rightarrow\langle\text{RV}(c)\rangle

implies
(\sigma,k_{1})\models\langle\text{RV}(c)\rangle
,
and thus
\text{RV}(c)\cap\mathcal{RV}\subseteq S_{1}
.
Let
S_{2}\triangleq\mathbf{dom}(k_{2})\cap\text{FV}(\eta)
.
Then,
\text{MV}(c)
is disjoint from
S_{2}
because
S_{2}\subseteq\text{FV}(\eta)
and
\text{FV}(\eta)\cap\text{MV}(c)=\emptyset
;
also, by restriction,
(\sigma,\pi_{S_{2}}k_{2})\models\eta
.

Since
k_{1}\oplus k_{2}
is non-empty,
dom(k_{1}),dom(k_{2})
are disjoint;
since
S_{1}=dom(k_{1}),S_{2}\subseteq dom(k_{2})
,
S_{1},S_{2}
are disjoint.

Let
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})=\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)
.
Denote
\text{RV}(c)\cap\mathcal{DV}
as
R_{1}
,
\text{MV}(C)\cap\mathcal{DV}
as
M_{1}
,

\text{RV}(c)\cap\mathcal{RV}
as
R_{2}
,
\text{MV}(C)\cap\mathcal{RV}
as
M_{2}
.
By the soundness of
\text{RV},\text{WV}
, and
MV
,
there exists
G:\mathbf{Mem}[R_{1}]\to\mathbf{Mem}[M_{1}]
,

F:\mathbf{Mem}[\text{RV}(c)]\to\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[M_{2}])
such that:

\displaystyle\sigma_{e}

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma)\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{\mathcal{DV}\setminus M_{1}}\sigma

\displaystyle\mu_{e}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}(\mu,m\mapsto F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m)\otimes\mathbf{unit}(\mathbf{p}_{\mathbf{dom}(\mu)\setminus M_{2}}m)).

Since
R_{2}\subseteq S_{1}
, and
S_{2}
is disjoint of
S_{1}
and
\text{MV}(c)
,
we have

(10)

\displaystyle\pi_{M_{2}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu_{e}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}(\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu,(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m_{1})\otimes\mathbf{unit}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{1}\setminus M_{2}}m_{1})\otimes\mathbf{unit}(m_{2})).

One implication is that
(\sigma_{e},\pi_{S_{2}}\mu_{e})\models\eta
:

Equation

10
implies that
\pi_{S_{2}}\mu_{e}=\pi_{S_{2}}\mu
.
We
(\sigma,\pi_{S_{2}}\mu)\models\eta
, so
(\sigma,\pi_{S_{2}}\mu_{e})\models\eta
.
By restriction, for any
m\in\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}\setminus\text{FV}(\eta)]
,

(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\eta)}\sigma\bowtie m,\pi_{S_{2}}\mu_{e})\models\eta
.
Since
\sigma_{e}=G(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma)\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{\mathcal{DV}\setminus M_{1}}\sigma
,
and
\text{FV}(\eta)\cap\text{MV}(c)=\emptyset
implies that
\text{FV}(\eta)\cap\mathcal{DV}\subseteq\mathcal{DV}\setminus M_{1}
,
and
(\sigma_{e},\pi_{S_{2}}\mu_{e})\models\eta
.

If
y\in\mathcal{DV}
, then
\llbracket y\rrbracket(\sigma_{e},m_{F}\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{X\cap\mathcal{RV}}m)=\sigma_{e}(y)
.
Thus,
(\sigma_{e},t)\models\langle y\rangle
where
t
is the trivial distribution in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Mem}[\emptyset])
.
Also,
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\in(\sigma_{e},t)\circ(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})
,
where
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\sqsupseteq(\sigma_{e},\pi_{S_{2}}\mu_{e})\models\eta
.
So
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\models\eta
.

If
y\notin\text{MV}(c)
, then
\{y\}\cup S_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{dom}(\mu)\setminus\text{MV}(c)
,
and thus
\pi_{\{y\}\cup S_{2}}\mu_{e}=\pi_{\{y\}\cup S_{2}}\mu
.

•:

Since
\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{c}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{{y}\sim{f(X)}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}
, by inductive assumption we have
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,k_{1})\models{y}\sim{f(X)}
.
Updates to deterministic variables only depend on deterministic program state, so
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,k_{1})=(\sigma_{e},\mu_{k})
for some
\mu_{k}
.
And
y\notin\text{MV}(c)
implies that
\pi_{\{y\}}\mu_{k}=\pi_{\{y\}}\mu=\pi_{\{y\}}k_{1}
.
The restriction property and
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,k_{1})\models{y}\sim{f(X)}
implies
(\sigma_{e},\pi_{\{y\}}k_{1})=(\sigma_{e},\pi_{\{y\}}\mu_{k})\models\langle y\rangle
.

•:

(\sigma_{e},\pi_{S_{2}}\mu)\models\eta
.

•:

\mu\sqsupseteq\mu^{\prime}\in k_{1}\oplus k_{2}
, so
\pi_{\{y\}\cup S_{2}}\mu\in\pi_{\{y\}}k_{1}\oplus\pi_{S_{2}}k_{2}
too.

Therefore,
(\sigma_{e},\pi_{\{y\}\cup S_{2}}\mu)\models\langle y\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\eta
.
Since
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\sqsupseteq(\sigma_{e},\pi_{\{y\}\cup S_{2}}\mu_{e})=(\sigma_{e},\pi_{\{y\}\cup S_{2}}\mu)
,
by persistence,
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\models\langle y\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\eta
.

If
y\in\mathcal{RV}
and
y\in\text{MV}(c)
,
our overall strategy is to first connect
F
with
f
and show the operation on variable
y
is a monotone map,
and then apply monotone map closure to establish the NA between
y
and
\eta
.

Since
(\sigma,\pi_{S_{1}}\mu)=(\sigma,k_{1})\models\phi
, by persistence
(\sigma,\mu)\models\phi
.
By side-condition that
\vdash\{\phi\}c\{{y}\sim{f(X)}\}
and by induction
that the proof rules are sound, it must
\llbracket c\rrbracket(\sigma,\mu)=(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\models{y}\sim{f(X)}
. By restriction,
(\sigma_{e},\pi_{X\cup\{y\}}\mu_{e})\models{y}\sim{f(X)}
.

Since
X\cap\mathcal{RV}\subseteq(\text{RV}(c)\cap\mathcal{RV})\setminus\text{MV}(c)\subseteq S_{1}\setminus\text{MV}(c)
and
y\in\text{MV}(c)
,

\displaystyle\pi_{X\cup\{y\}}\mu_{e}

\displaystyle=\pi_{X\cup\{y\}}\pi_{S_{1}\cup\text{MV}(c)}\llbracket c\rrbracket\mu

\displaystyle=\pi_{X\cup\{y\}}\mathbf{bind}(\pi_{S_{1}}\mu,m\mapsto F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m)\otimes\mathbf{unit}(\mathbf{p}_{S_{1}\setminus M_{2}}m))

(11)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}(\pi_{S_{1}}\mu,m\mapsto\pi_{\{y\}}F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m)\otimes\mathbf{unit}(\mathbf{p}_{X\cap\mathcal{RV}}m))

Since
(\sigma_{e},\pi_{X\cup\{y\}}\mu_{e})\models{y}\sim{f(X)}
, for every
m_{e}
in the support of
\pi_{X\cup\{y\}}\mu_{e}
,
we have
\llbracket y\rrbracket(\sigma_{e},m_{e})=\llbracket f(X)\rrbracket(\sigma_{e},m_{e})
.
By
Equation

11
, a memory
m_{e}
is in the support of
\pi_{X\cup\{y\}}\mu_{e}
if and only if there exists some
m,m_{F}

such that
m
is in the support of
\mu
, and
m_{F}
is in the support of
\pi_{\{y\}}F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m)
,
and
m_{e}=m_{F}\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{X\cap\mathcal{RV}}m
.
Thus, the condition we have is:
for every
m
is in the support of
\mu
and
m_{F}
is in the support of
\pi_{\{y\}}F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m)
,

\llbracket y\rrbracket(\sigma_{e},m_{F}\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{X\cap\mathcal{RV}}m)=\llbracket f(X)\rrbracket(\sigma_{e},m_{F}\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{X\cap\mathcal{RV}}m).

Since
f
does not depend on states and
X
do not depend on
m_{F}
,
we also have that
\llbracket f(X)\rrbracket(\sigma_{e},m_{F}\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{X\cap\mathcal{RV}}m)=\llbracket f(X)\rrbracket(\sigma,m)
.

If
y\in\mathcal{RV}
, then
\llbracket y\rrbracket(\sigma_{e},m_{F}\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{X\cap\mathcal{RV}}m)=m_{F}(y)
,
so it must
m_{F}(y)=\llbracket f(X)\rrbracket(\sigma,m)
.
so although
F
is a randomized function according to its type,
for any
m
in the support of
\mu
,

\pi_{\{y\}}F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m)
is a Dirac distribution:

\pi_{\{y\}}F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m)=\delta(\llbracket f(X)\rrbracket(\sigma,m)).

Fixing
\sigma
, then there exists
f^{\prime}
such that
\llbracket f(X)\rrbracket(\sigma,m)=f^{\prime}(\mathbf{p}_{X\cap\mathcal{RV}}m)

and
f^{\prime}
is monotone as
f
is monotone. Since
X\cap\mathcal{RV}\subseteq S_{1}
,
we can also make
f^{\prime}
to have type
\mathbf{Mem}[S_{1}]\to\mathbf{Val}
.

Thus,

\displaystyle\pi_{\{y\}\cup S_{2}}\mu_{e}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}(\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu,(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto\pi_{y}F(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{R_{2}}m_{1})\otimes\mathbf{unit}(m_{2}))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}(\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu,(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto\mathbf{unit}(\llbracket f(X)\rrbracket(\sigma,m_{1}))\otimes\mathbf{unit}(m_{2}))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}(\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu,(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto\mathbf{unit}(f^{\prime}(m_{1}))\otimes\mathbf{unit}(m_{2})).

Let
\mu_{c}=\pi_{\{y\}\cup S_{2}}(\mu_{e})
.
We will then show that

(\sigma_{e},\mu_{c})\models\langle y\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\eta.

Let
g_{1}=\pi_{y}(\mu_{e})
,
g_{2}=\pi_{S_{2}}\mu
,
it suffices to show that
\mu_{c}\in g_{1}\oplus g_{2}
.
and
g_{1}\models\langle y\rangle
,

g_{2}\models\eta
:

•:

(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\models{y}\sim{f(X)}
, so
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\models\langle y\rangle
.
By restriction,
(\sigma_{e},g_{1})\models\langle y\rangle
.

•:

g_{2}=\pi_{S_{2}}\mu=\pi_{FV(\eta)}\pi_{dom(k_{2})}\mu=\pi_{FV(\eta)}k_{2}
,
so
(\sigma,g_{2})\models\eta
.
By
Lemma

D.6
, for any
m\in\mathbf{Mem}[\mathcal{DV}\setminus\text{FV}(\eta)]
,

(\mathbf{p}_{\text{FV}(\eta)}\sigma\bowtie m,g_{2})\models\eta
.
Since
\sigma_{e}=G(\mathbf{p}_{R_{1}}\sigma)\bowtie\mathbf{p}_{\mathcal{DV}\setminus M_{1}}\sigma
,
and
\text{FV}(\eta)\cap\text{MV}(c)=\emptyset
implies that
\text{FV}(\eta)\subseteq\mathcal{DV}\setminus M_{1}
,

(\sigma_{e},g_{2})\models\eta
.

•:

First,

\pi_{\{y\}}\mu_{c}=\pi_{\{y\}}\mu_{e}=g_{1}
,
and
\pi_{S_{2}}\mu_{c}=\pi_{S_{2}}\mu_{e}=\pi_{S_{2}}\mu=g_{2}
.

Second,
\pi_{dom(k_{1})\cup dom(k_{2})}\mu\in k_{1}\oplus k_{2}

implies that
\pi_{dom(k_{1})\cup dom(k_{2})}\mu
is

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
-
PNA
for any
\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
such that
k_{1}
is
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
-
PNA
,
k_{2}
is
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
-
PNA
.
Because
\pi_{S_{1}}\mu
is always
\{S_{1}\}
-
PNA
,

\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu
is
\{S_{1}\}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
for any
\mathcal{T}
such that
g_{2}
is
\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
.
Recall that

\displaystyle\mu_{c}=\mathbf{bind}(\pi_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\mu,(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto\mathbf{unit}(f^{\prime}(m_{1}))\otimes\mathbf{unit}(m_{2})).

Thus, by the monotonicity map closure
Lemma

D.9
,

\mu_{c}
is
\{y\}\cup\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
for any
\mathcal{T}
such that
g_{2}
is
\mathcal{T}
-
PNA
.
Thus,
\mu_{c}\in g_{1}\oplus g_{2}
, and therefore
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{c})\in(\sigma_{e},g_{1})\oplus(\sigma_{e},g_{2})
.

Therefore,
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{c})\models\langle y\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\eta
.

By persistence,
(\sigma_{e},\mu_{e})\models\langle y\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\eta
.

∎

Appendix E
Completeness of
M
-BI

E.1.
M
-BI Algebras

Definition E.1 (BI Algebra).

An BI algebra is an algebra
\mathcal{A}=(A,\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\top,\bot,\mathrel{\ast},\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*},\top^{\mathrel{\ast}})
such that

•

(A,\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\top,\bot)
is a Heyting algebra

•

(A,\mathrel{\ast},\top^{\mathrel{\ast}})
is a commutative monoid

•

a\mathrel{\ast}b\leq c
if and only if
a\leq b\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}c

where
\leq
is the ordering associated with the Heyting algebra.

Definition E.2 (
M
-BI Algebra).

An
M
-BI algebra is an algebra
\mathcal{A}=(A,\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\top,\bot,\mathrel{\ast}_{m\in M},\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m\in M},\top^{\mathrel{\ast}}_{m\in M})
such that

•

For each
m\in M
, the structure
(A,\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\top,\bot,\mathrel{\ast}_{m},\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m},\top^{\mathrel{\ast}}_{m})
is a BI-algebra

•

If
m_{1}\leq m_{2}
then
a\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}b\leq a\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}b

We can interpret
M
-BI in an
M
-BI algebra
A
. Let
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{AP}\rightarrow A
be a map assigning atomic propositions to elements
of
A
. We extend
\mathcal{V}
to an interpretation
\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

mapping propositions to elements of
A
, defined by:

\displaystyle\llbracket p\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(p)

\displaystyle\llbracket\top\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\top

\displaystyle\llbracket I_{m}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\top^{\mathrel{\ast}}_{m}

\displaystyle\llbracket\bot\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\bot

\displaystyle\llbracket P\land Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\land\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\llbracket P\lor Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\lor\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\llbracket P\rightarrow Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\llbracket P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\mathrel{\ast}_{m}\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\llbracket P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

Theorem E.3 (Algebraic Soundness).

If
P\vdash Q
is provable, then for all
\mathcal{V}
,
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
P\vdash Q
. The cases for everything except the
Inclusion
rules show follow from the exact same argument as for standard BI and BI-algebra, as in Simon Docherty’s thesis.

For the remaining case of
\mathrel{\ast}
-
inclusion
, let
m_{1}\leq m_{2}
. Then we have

\llbracket P\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}=\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\llbracket P\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}

∎

Definition E.4 (Lindenbaum-Tarski Algebra).

The Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra corresponding to
M
-BI is the set of all equivalence classes of interprovable propositions. That is, define the equivalence relation
P\sim Q
as
P\vdash Q
and
Q\vdash P
. We will show that the set of equivalence classes of this relation forms an
M
-BI algebra. Let
[P]_{\sim}
be the equivalence class of
P
under
\sim
.
Take
I_{m}
,
\top
, and
\bot
to be
[I_{m}]_{\sim}
,
[\top]_{\sim}
, and
[\bot]_{\sim}
, respectively. Then we define:

\displaystyle[P]_{\sim}\land[Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[P\land Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle[P]_{\sim}\lor[Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[P\lor Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle[P]_{\sim}\mathrel{\ast}_{m}[Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[P\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle[P]_{\sim}\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}[Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[P\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}Q]_{\sim}

The fact that these operations are well-defined and form a
M
-BI algebra follows almost entirely from the corresponding result for normal BI outlined in Docherty’s thesis. The only remaining case is to check that if
m_{1}\leq m_{2}
then
[P]_{\sim}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}[Q]_{\sim}\leq[P]_{\sim}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}[Q]_{\sim}
. We have

\displaystyle[P]_{\sim}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}[Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[P\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}Q]_{\sim}

(Since
P\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}Q\leq P\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}Q
)

\displaystyle\leq[P\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}Q]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[P]_{\sim}\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}[Q]_{\sim}

Lemma E.5.

P\vdash Q
if and only if
[P]_{\sim}\leq[Q]_{\sim}
.

Proof.

In the proof that the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra indeed formed an
M
-BI algebra, we already showed that
P\vdash Q
implies
[P]_{\sim}\leq[Q]_{\sim}
. Consider the opposite direction. Then we have that
[P]_{\sim}\land[Q]_{\sim}=[P]_{\sim}
, hence
[P\land Q]_{\sim}=[P]_{\sim}
. This implies that
P\land Q\dashv\vdash P
. Since
P\land Q\vdash Q
, by transitivity we have
P\vdash Q
.
∎

Theorem E.6 (Algebraic Completeness).

If
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}
for all
\mathcal{V}
, then
P\vdash Q
.

Proof.

Consider the valuation
\mathcal{V}
which maps
p\in\mathcal{AP}
to
[p]_{\sim}
. Then
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}=[P]_{\sim}
and
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}}=[Q]_{\sim}
. Hence we have

[P]_{\sim}\leq[Q]_{\sim}
which implies
P\vdash Q
.
∎

E.2.
M
-BI Frames

M
-BI formulas are interpreted on Down-Closed
M
-BI frames.
We define a complex algebra on
M
-BI frames.

Definition E.7 (Complex Algebra).

If
\mathcal{X}
is an
M
-BI frame, then the
complex algebra
of
\mathcal{X}
, written
\textsf{Com}(\mathcal{X})
is the structure
(\mathcal{P}_{\sqsubseteq}(X),\cap,\cup,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{X}},X,\emptyset,\mathrel{\ast}_{m\in M},\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m\in M},E_{m\in M})
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\sqsubseteq}(X)

\displaystyle=\{A\subseteq X\ |\ a\in A\land a\sqsubseteq b\rightarrow b\in A\}

\displaystyle A\rightarrow_{\mathcal{X}}B

\displaystyle=\{a\ |\ \forall b.\,a\sqsubseteq b\land b\in A\rightarrow b\in B\}

\displaystyle A\mathrel{\ast}_{m}B

\displaystyle=\{x\ |\ \exists w,y,z.\,w\sqsubseteq x\land w\in y\oplus_{m}z\land y\in A\land z\in B\}

\displaystyle A\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}B

\displaystyle=\{x\ |\ \forall w,y,z.\,(x\sqsubseteq w\land z\in w\oplus_{m}y\land y\in A)\rightarrow z\in B\}

Lemma E.8.

If
\mathcal{X}
is an
M
-BI frame, then
\textsf{Com}(\mathcal{X})
is an
M
-BI algebra.

Proof.

Each
(X,\sqsubseteq,\oplus_{m},E_{m})
is a
BI frame.
Docherty 2019
shows that the complex of a BI frame is a
BI algebra. Thus the only thing to check is that the ordering on
\mathrel{\ast}

respects the ordering on
M
. Let
m_{1}\leq m_{2}
. We must show
that
A\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}B\subseteq A\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}B
. Let
x\in A\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}B
. Then there exists
w,y,z
such that
w\sqsubseteq x

and
w\in y\oplus_{m_{1}}z
, with
y\in A
and
z\in B
.
by the
Operation Inclusion
property, we have that
w\in y\oplus_{m_{2}}z
, hence

x\in A\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}B
.
∎

Definition E.9 (Prime Filter).

If
(L,\land,\lor)
is a bounded distributive lattice, a prime filter on
F
is a non-empty proper subset of
A
such that:

•

If
x\in F
and
x\leq y
then
y\in F
.

•

If
x\in F
and
y\in F
then
x\land y\in F
.

•

If
x\lor y\in F
then
x\in F
or
y\in F
.

We write
\textsf{Prf}(L)
for the set of prime filters on
L
.

Definition E.10 (Prime Filter Frame).

If
\mathcal{A}
is an
M
-BI algebra, then the prime filter
M
-frame of
\mathcal{A}
is defined as
\textsf{Prf}(\mathcal{A})=(\textsf{Prf}(A),\subseteq,\oplus_{m\in M},E_{m\in M})

where

\displaystyle F_{1}\oplus_{m}F_{2}

\displaystyle=\{F\in\textsf{Prf}(A)\ |\ \forall a_{1}\in F_{1}.\,\forall a_{2}\in F_{2}.\,a_{1}\mathrel{\ast}_{m}a_{2}\in F\}

\displaystyle E_{m}

\displaystyle=\{F\in\textsf{Prf}(A)\ |\ \top^{\mathrel{\ast}}_{m}\in F\}

Lemma E.11.

If
\mathcal{A}
is an
M
-BI algebra, then
\textsf{Prf}(\mathcal{A})
is an
M
-BI frame.

Proof.

Docherty 2019
shows that for each
m\in M
,
(\textsf{Prf}(A),\subseteq,\oplus_{m},E_{m})
is a BI frame. Therefore, we only need to check the Operation Inclusion property. Let
m_{1}\leq m_{2}

and let
F,G,H\in\textsf{Prf}(A)
with
F\in G\oplus_{m_{1}}H
.
Let
a\in G
and
b\in H
. Then
a\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}b\in F
. Since
a\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{1}}b\leq a\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}b
, and filters are upward-closed,

a\mathrel{\ast}_{m_{2}}b\in F
, hence
F\in G\oplus_{m_{2}}H
.

∎

Theorem E.12 (Representation Theorem).

Every
M
-BI algebra is isomorphic to a subalgebra of a complex algebra. In particular, if
\mathcal{A}
is an
M
-BI algebra,
then the map
\theta:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\textsf{Com}(\textsf{Prf}(\mathcal{A}))
defined as

\theta(x)=\{F\in\textsf{Prf}(\mathcal{A})\ |\ x\in F\}

is an embedding.

Proof.

Docherty 2019
proves that for each
m\in M
, this map
\theta
is an embedding of
(A,\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\top,\bot,\mathrel{\ast}_{m},\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m},\top^{\mathrel{\ast}}_{m})
as a BI algebra into the complex algebra, viewed as a BI algebra for the operations indexed by
m
. Hence,
\theta
is injective and a homomorphism with respect to all of the
M
-BI algebra operations.
∎

Theorem E.13 (Equivalence of Algebras and Frames).

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A,\dots)
be an
M
-BI algebra and let
\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}:\mathcal{AP}\rightarrow A
be an interpretation of atomic propositions. Let
\mathcal{X}=(X,\dots)
be an
M
-BI frame and let
\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}:\textsf{Prop}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(X)
be a persistent valuation on
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\theta
be the embedding from the previous result. Define the persistent valuation
\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}:\mathcal{AP}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\textsf{Prf}(A))
and the interpretation
\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}^{\prime}:\mathcal{AP}\rightarrow\textsf{Com}(\mathcal{X})
by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(p)

\displaystyle=\theta(\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}(p))

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}^{\prime}(p)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}(p).

Then we have

(1)

x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}P
if and only if
x\in\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}^{\prime}}

(2)

F\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}P
if and only if
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}}\in F
.

Proof.

For the first part, we proceed by induction on
P
.

•

Case
P=p
: We have:

\displaystyle x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}p

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}(p)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f'}}(p)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\textsf{f'}}

•

Case
P=\top
: Then
x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}\top
holds for all
x
, and
\top
in
\textsf{Com}(\mathcal{X})
is defined to be
X
, so
x\in\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}^{\prime}}
holds for all
x
.

•

Case
P=I_{m}
: Similar to
P=\top
.

•

Case
P=\bot
: Similar to
P=\top
.

•

Case
P=Q_{1}\land Q_{2}
:

(By satisfication rule)

\displaystyle x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}\land Q_{2}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}\textrm{ and }x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{2}

(
\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}
the same)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}\textrm{ and }x\in\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}\cap\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}

(By the
\land
operation in Complex algebra and the recursive definition of
\mathcal{V}
)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\land Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}

•

Case
P=Q_{1}\lor Q_{2}
:

\displaystyle x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}\lor Q_{2}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}\textrm{ or }x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{2}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}\textrm{ or }x\in\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}\cup\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\lor Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}

•

Case
P=Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}
: Let
x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}
. Then, for all
y
such that
x\sqsubseteq y
,
if
y\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}
, then
y\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{2}
. Applying the induction hypothesis, we have that for all
y
such that
x\sqsubseteq y
,
if
y\in\llbracket Q_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
, then

y\in\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
.
Hence,
x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
. The reverse direction is similar.

•

Case
P=Q_{1}\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q_{2}
: Let
x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q_{2}
. Then there exists
x^{\prime}
,
y
, and
z
such that
x^{\prime}\sqsubseteq x
and
x^{\prime}\in y\oplus_{m}z
, where
y\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}
and
z\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{2}
. By the induction hypothesis, we have that
y\in\llbracket Q_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
and
z\in\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
. Hence,
x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\mathrel{\ast}_{m}Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
.

•

Case
P=Q_{1}\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}Q_{2}
: Let
x\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}Q_{2}
. Then for all
x^{\prime}
,
y
, and
z
such that
x\sqsubseteq x^{\prime}
and
z\in x^{\prime}\oplus_{m}y
, if
y\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}
, then
z\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{2}
.

To show that
x\in\llbracket Q_{1}\mathrel{-\mkern-6.0mu*}_{m}Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
, let
w
,
y
, and
z
be such that
x\sqsubseteq w
,
z\in w\oplus_{m}y
, and
y\in\llbracket Q_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
. We must show that
z\in\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
. Applying the induction hypothesis, we have that
y\in\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{1}
. Thus, by the preceding paragraph, we have that
z\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}}}Q_{2}
. Applying the induction hypothesis again, we get that
z\in\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{f}^{\prime}}}
.

For the second part, assuming
F\in\textsf{Prf}(\mathcal{A})
,
then for any
P
, we have

\displaystyle F\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}P

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow F\in\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}^{\prime}}(P)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow F\in\textsf{Prf}(\mathcal{A})\text{ and }\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}(P)\in F

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}(P)\in F

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}}\in F.

∎

Theorem E.14 (Completeness).

If
P\models_{\mathcal{V}}Q
for all
\mathcal{V}
, then
P\vdash Q

Proof.

Suppose
P\not\vdash Q
. Then by algebraic completeness, there exists some

M
-BI algebra
\mathcal{A}
and an interpretation

\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}
such that
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}}\nleq\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}}
. By the prime filter theorem,
there exists
(
Docherty 2019
)
a prime filter
F
such that
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}}\in F

and
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}}\not\in F
. Let
\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}
be as in the previous theorem, then we have

F\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}P
and
F\not\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}Q
which contradicts the assumption that
P\models_{\mathcal{V}_{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}Q
.
∎

Appendix F
Examples: Omitted Details

F.1.
Bound false positive rate in Bloom filter

One detail we omitted is that, since the first line of the program
Bloom
,

bloom
has been kept as a
bit-array
throughout, i.e.,
all its entries are either 0 or 1. So it is easy to prove that

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\top}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{Bloom}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{N}(bloom[\beta]=0\lor bloom[\beta]=1)}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Then, by the conjunction rule
Conj
, we have

\displaystyle\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\top}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{Bloom}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr\left[\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]<K+T(\delta,N)\right]\geq 1-\delta}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}},

where
K=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]\right]
.

In the following, we will abbreviate formulas that assert

b
is a bit-array where exactly
J
of its first
N
entries
are one,

\left(\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}b[\beta]=J\right)\land\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{N}(b[\beta]=0\lor b[\beta]=1),

as
\mathbf{bv}(b,J,N)
.
Similarly, we will use
\mathbf{bv}(b,<J,N)
to abbreviate

\left(\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}b[\beta]<J\right)\land\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{N}(b[\beta]=0\lor b[\beta]=1).

Now we restate our goal as

\displaystyle\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathbf{bv}(bloom,<K,N)}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{CheckMem}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{H}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

CheckMem
first initializes
h
and
allhit
deterministically to 1.
Then, using
RAssn
and
Frame
, we can show that

\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\top}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{h}\leftarrow{0};{allhit}\leftarrow{1}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\left({h}\sim{0}\right)\mathrel{\ast}\left({allhit}\sim{1}\right)}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Using the (
ProbOne
) axiom and the fact that
1\leq(K/N)^{0}
for any
K
and
N
,
we can show
\models\left({h}\sim{0}\right)\mathrel{\ast}\left({allhit}\sim{1}\right)\to\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{h}
.
Thus,

\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{720242}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\top}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{720242}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{h}\leftarrow{0};{allhit}\leftarrow{1}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{720242}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{h}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{720242}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Because the assignments
{h}\leftarrow{0};{allhit}\leftarrow{1}
do not modify the
Bloom filter array
bloom
, we can then apply the frame rule
Frame
to
derive

(12)

\displaystyle\vdash\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{720242}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathbf{bv}(bloom,<K,N)}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{720242}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{h}\leftarrow{0};{allhit}\leftarrow{1}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{720242}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathbf{bv}(bloom,<K,N)\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{h}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{720242}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

We will abbreviate
\mathbf{bv}(bloom,<K,N)\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{h}
as
\eta
.
Because
\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}b[\beta]
is an integer upper bounded by
N
,

\models_{\mathbf{Mem}}\eta\to\bigvee_{0\leq J<K}\eta_{J},

where
\eta_{J}
abbreviates
J<K\land\left(\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{h}\right)
.

We will then prove that for each
J
, the formula
\eta_{J}

is a loop invariant of
CheckMem
’s loop body.
The loop body first uniformly sample an element from
[N]
,
so by
RSamp
∗
,

(13)

\displaystyle\eta_{J}\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle bin\rangle.

Together with the axiom
\models P\mathrel{\ast}Q\to P
,
Equation

13

implies

J<K\land\left(\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\left(\Pr[allhit]\leq(\frac{K}{N})^{h}\right)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle bin\rangle\right).

Then,
hit
gets assigned to
bloom[bin]
, so by
RAssn
, we have

\hbox{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{\resizebox{282168}{724537}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle bin\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{\resizebox{282168}{724537}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{hit}\leftarrow{bloom[\beta]}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{\resizebox{282168}{724537}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\left(\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle bin\rangle\right)\land{hit}\sim{bloom[bin]}}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{\resizebox{282168}{724537}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Since the array
bloom
only contains zero-one entries, when the sum of its entries is
J
,
an entry
bloom[bin]
drawn uniformly at random has probability
\frac{J}{N}
to be 1.
If the entry is in addition chosen independently from values in
bloom
,
then the bit
bloom[bin]
is distributed independent from the distribution of
bloom
.
The (
UniformSamp
) axiom encodes this fact:

\models\left(\left(\mathbf{bv}(b,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle x\rangle\right)\land{hit}\sim{b[x]}\right)\to\mathbf{Bern}_{\frac{J}{N}}\langle hit\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{bv}(b,J,N).

Thus, we have

\hbox{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{\resizebox{282168}{724537}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle bin\rangle}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{\resizebox{282168}{724537}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{hit}\leftarrow{bloom[\beta]}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{\resizebox{282168}{724537}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathbf{Bern}_{\frac{J}{N}}\langle hit\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)}\hbox{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{\resizebox{282168}{724537}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Because
{hit}\leftarrow{bloom[bin]}
does not modify
allhit
, we
can apply the frame rule for
\mathrel{\ast}

Frame
and get

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle bin\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{h}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad{hit}\leftarrow{bloom[\beta]}

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{Bern}_{\frac{J}{N}}\langle hit\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{h}\right\}.

Next, with the assignment
{allhit}\leftarrow{hit\mathop{\&\&}allhit}
,
by applying the axioms (
IndepProb
), (
EqualProb
)
and the
RAssn
rule,
we get:

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{Bern}_{\frac{J}{N}}\langle hit\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{h}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad{allhit}\leftarrow{hit\mathop{\&\&}allhit}

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\Pr[allhit]\leq\frac{J}{N}\cdot\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{h}\right)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\right\}

We can then apply the rule of constancy
Const
and get

\displaystyle\left\{J<K\land\left(\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle bin\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{h}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad{hit}\leftarrow{bloom[\beta]};{allhit}\leftarrow{hit\mathop{\&\&}allhit}

\displaystyle\left\{J<K\land\left(\left(\Pr[allhit]\leq\frac{J}{N}\cdot\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{h}\right)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\right)\right\}

When we have
J<K
, then
(K/N)^{h}\cdot\frac{J}{N}\leq(K/N)^{h+1}
, so the post condition
implies

J<K\land\left(\left(\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{h+1}\right)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\right)

The last step in the loop body is the assignment
{h}\leftarrow{h+1}
.
By the deterministic assignment rule
DAssn
,
we can establish the post condition
\eta_{J}
afterwards:

J<K\land\left(\mathbf{bv}(bloom,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{h}\right).

Thus, we have
\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{273066}{409599}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\eta_{J}}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{273066}{409599}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textit{loop body}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{273066}{409599}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\eta_{J}}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{273066}{409599}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

By
Loop
rule, we can establish
\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\eta_{J}}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{loop}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\eta_{J}\land h\geq H}\hbox{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{282168}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}
.
Since
\eta_{J}\land h\geq H
implies
\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{H}
, we then have

\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\eta_{J}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{loop}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{H}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Because
\Pr[allhit]\leq(K/N)^{H}
is closed under mixtures,
and
\eta
is closed under conditioning, we can then apply
RCase
to prove that

(14)

\displaystyle\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\eta}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{loop}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{H}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{715146}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

Using the
Seqn
rule to combine the proved judgments for
CheckMem
’s
initialization (
12
) and loop (
14
), we derive

\displaystyle\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}bloom[\beta]<K}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{CheckMem}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[allhit]\leq\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{H}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}.

F.2.
Permutation Hashing

We sketch how to replicate the informal reasoning in
LINA
. For the main
loop, we apply the rule
Loop
with the following loop invariant:

\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{n}{hitZ[\alpha]}\sim{[mod(g[\alpha],B)=Z]}\land\mathbf{Perm}_{[B\cdot K]}\langle g\rangle\land ct=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}hitZ[\alpha]\land(\neg(n<N)\to{n}\sim{N})

The loop invariant is preserved by the body of the loop, using the assignment
rule (
RAssn
) and the rule of constancy (
Const
). Thus we can
show the following judgment:

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{{ct}\sim{0}\land{n}\sim{0}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{loop}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{N}{hitZ[\alpha]}\sim{[mod(g[\alpha],B)=Z]}\land\mathbf{Perm}_{[B\cdot K]}\langle g\rangle\land{ct}\sim{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}hitZ[\alpha]}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

Applying (
Perm-Map
), the post-condition implies:

\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{N}{hitZ[\alpha]}\sim{[mod(g[\alpha],B)=Z]}\land\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle hitZ[\alpha]\rangle\land\mathbf{Perm}_{[B\cdot K]}\langle g\rangle\land{ct}\sim{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}hitZ[\alpha]}

Applying basic axioms about expected value and the permutation distribution
((
PermMarg
) (
ProbUnif
) (
BijectUnif
)), we have:

\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle hitZ[\alpha]\rangle\land{ct}\sim{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}hitZ[\alpha]}\land{\mathbb{E}[ct]}\sim{N/B}

And we can apply the negative-association Chernoff bound (
NA-Chernoff
)
to conclude:

\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\top}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{PermHash}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[|ct-N/B|>T(\beta,N)]<\beta}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

This conclusion corresponds to Proposition A.2 in

Ding and
König 2011
algorithm for fast set
intersection.
6
6

6

Ding and
König 2011
apply a variant of the Chernoff bound to
obtain a multiplicative, rather than an additive, error guarantee. We present
the additive version since the bound is a bit simpler, but there is no
difficulty to handling the multiplicative version in our framework.

F.3.
Fully-Dynamic Dictionary

We outline the main steps in the formal proof; the most interesting step is the
last one, where we use negative association, but all steps can be handled in our
framework.

We will refer to the two outer-most loops as (1) and (2), the next two
outer-most loops as (1.1) and (2.1), and the inner-most loop as (1.1.1).

Computing
\mathbb{E}[binCt[c][p]]
.

For loop (1), we apply
Loop
with the following loop invariant:

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{P}{\mathbb{E}[binCt[\gamma][\beta]]}\sim{n/(P\cdot C)}\land(\neg(n<N)\to{n}\sim{N}).

To show that this invariant is preserved by the loop, by two applications of

RSamp*
the following holds after the sampling commands:

\mathbf{OH}_{[P]}\langle pocket[n]\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{OH}_{[C]}\langle crate[n]\rangle\land{bin[n]}\sim{crate[n]^{\top}\cdot pocket[n]}.

Using an axiom about independence and products of one-hot vectors
(
IndProdOH
), this implies:

\mathbf{OH}_{[C]\times[P]}\langle bin[n]\rangle.

Using an axiom about the one-hot encoding (
OHMarg
):

{\mathbb{E}[bin[\alpha][\gamma][\beta]]}\sim{1/(P\cdot C)}

for every
\alpha
,
\gamma
, and
\beta
. Standard loop invariants for loop
(1.1) and (1.1.1) show that:

{binCt[c][p]}\sim{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}bin[\alpha][c][p]},

and linearity of expectation establishes the invariant condition for loop (1).
The invariant holds at the start of loop (1) since
binCt
is zero-initialized,
and it also holds at the end of loop (1). Since
binCt
is not modified further,
the expectation bound also holds at the end of the program (
Const
).

Bounding
\Pr[binCt[c][p]>T_{bin}]
.

For loop (1), we apply
Loop
with the following loop invariant:

\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{n}\langle bin[\alpha]\rangle\right)\land\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{P}{binCt[\gamma][\beta]}\sim{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}bin[\alpha][\gamma][\beta]}\land(\neg(n<N)\to{n}\sim{N}).

The first conjunction is an invariant, by applying the sampling rule

Samp*
and the independence frame rule
Frame
from PSL. The rest
of the invariant is preserved, following standard invariants for loops (1.1) and
(1.1.1). By projection (
IndMap
), at the end of loop (1) we can
conclude:

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{P}\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle bin[\alpha][\gamma][\beta]\rangle\right)\land{binCt[\gamma][\beta]}\sim{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}bin[\alpha][\gamma][\beta]}.

Thus a (standard) Chernoff bound gives:

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{P}\Pr[binCt[\gamma][\beta]>\mathbb{E}[binCt[\gamma][\beta]]+T(\rho_{bin},N)]\leq\rho_{bin}.

where
\mathbb{E}[binCt[\gamma][\beta]]
is
N/(P\cdot C)
by the previous step.
Again, property holds until the end of the program since
binCt
is not modified
further (
Const
).

Bounding
\mathbb{E}[overCt[c]]
.

Using standard loop invariants, at the end of loop (2) we have:

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}{overCt[\gamma]}\sim{\sum_{\beta=0}^{P}\sum_{\delta=0}^{C}bin[\delta][\beta]}\land\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{P}{over[\gamma][\beta]}\sim{[binCt[\gamma][\beta]>T_{bin}]}.

Using linearity of expectation and the fact that
over[\gamma][\beta]
is either
zero or one, we have:

\mathbb{E}[overCt[\gamma]]\sim\sum_{\beta=0}^{P}\mathbb{E}[over[\gamma][\beta]]\sim\sum_{\beta=0}^{P}\Pr[over[\gamma][\beta]=1]\sim\sum_{\beta=0}^{P}\Pr[binCt[\gamma][\beta]>T_{bin}]\leq P\cdot\rho_{bin}

since we have bound the probability in the previous step.

Bounding
\Pr[overCt[c]>T_{over}]
