.

We want the following loop invariant for (1):

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\bigwedge_{\beta=0}^{P}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\land(\neg(n<N)\to{n}\sim{N}).

We want the following loop invariant for (1.1):

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=c}^{P}\langle bin[n][\gamma][\beta]\rangle\land(\neg(p<P)\to{p}\sim{P}).

And the following loop invariant for (1.1.1):

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p+[c>\gamma]}^{P}\langle bin[n][\gamma][\beta]\rangle.

We show the invariant post-conditions for a fixed
\gamma
; the big conjunction
then follows by applying
Conj
. Working from inside-to-outside, we start
with loop (1.1.1). To establish the invariant condition, the critical case is
c=\gamma
. We can pull out:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{p}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p+1}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p+1}^{P}\langle bin[n][\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\underbrace{\langle binCt[\gamma][p]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\langle bin[n][\gamma][p]\rangle}_{\Phi}

Now, we can use the assignment rule to show:

\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Phi}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{{upd}\leftarrow{binCt[c][p]+bin[n][c][p]}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{{upd}\sim{binCt[c][p]+bin[n][c][p]}}\hbox{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{282168}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

Since addition is a monotone function, the NA frame rule (
NegFrame
)
gives:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{p}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p+1}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p+1}^{P}\langle bin[n][\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\langle upd\rangle

after the assignment to
upd
. After the assignment to
bin[c][p]
, we can fold:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p+1}^{P}\langle bin[n][\gamma][\beta]\rangle

to establish the invariant for loop (1.1.1).

To establish the invariant for loop (1.1), when the inner-most loop (1.1.1)
terminates we have
c>\gamma
, and so we have:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p+1}^{P}\langle bin[n][\gamma][\beta]\rangle.

To establish the invariant for loop (1), note that the invariant for loop (1.1)
holds on loop entry since
z
is zero-initialized (
DetInd
). And the
loop invariant for loop (1) is established when loop (1.1) exits, when
p=P
.

Next, we tackle loop (2). We take the invariant:

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{p}\langle over[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle.

For the inner loop (2.1), we take the invariant:

\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{p+[c>\gamma]}\langle over[\gamma][\beta]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=p+[c>\gamma]}^{P}\langle binCt[\gamma][\beta]\rangle.

Again, we show the invariant post-conditions for a fixed
\gamma
. For the
critical iteration
c=\gamma
, we again isolate
binCt[\gamma][p]
, observe
that addition is monotone and the function
[binCt[c][p]>T_{bin}]
is monotone in

binCt[\gamma][p]
, and apply the NA frame rule (
NegFrame
).

Finally at the end of the program, we can show:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\beta=0}^{P}\langle over[\gamma][\beta]\rangle

along with the regular invariant

{overCt[\gamma]}\sim{\sum_{\beta=0}^{P}over[\gamma][\beta]}.

We can then apply the negative-dependence Chernoff bound (
NA-Chernoff
):

\Pr[overCt[\gamma]>\mathbb{E}[overCt[\gamma]]+T(\rho_{over},P)]\leq\rho_{over}.

Using the expectation bound from the previous step and putting everything
together, we conclude:

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\top}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{FDDict}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\bigwedge_{\gamma=0}^{C}\Pr[overCt[\gamma]>P\cdot\rho_{bin}+T(\rho_{over},P)]\leq\rho_{over}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}},

thus showing a high-probability upper-bound on the number of overfull pocket
dictionaries within each crate.

F.4.
Repeated Balls-into-Bins

We will refer to the loops in
Figure

8(b)
using the same scheme we
used before: the outer-most loop is loop (1), the three next-outer-most loops
are loops (1.1), (1.2), and (1.3), and the inner-most loop is loop (1.2.1).
Starting from the outside, we take the following invariant for loop (1):

\Pr\left[\bigvee_{\beta=0}^{r}(emptyCt[\beta]>T_{empty})\right]\leq r\cdot\rho_{empty}\land{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}ct[\alpha]}\sim{N}

Showing the invariant condition requires some work. First, note that:

\models_{\mathbf{Mem}}{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}ct[\alpha]}\sim{N}\to\bigvee_{\sigma:[N]\to[N]}\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{|\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)|}

where
\sigma:[N]\to[N]
ranges over all assignments of
N
balls to
N

bins, and where we write
\models_{\mathbf{Mem}}
to denote that the formula is valid in
all
memories
, rather than distributions over memories. We write

\tau(\alpha)=|\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)|
for the number of balls in bin
\alpha
.
We will show:

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{\tau(\alpha)}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{body}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr\left[emptyCt[r]<T_{empty}\right]\leq\rho_{empty}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

where
body
is the body of loop (1). For loop (1.1), it is straightforward to
show the invariant using the loop rule
Loop
:

\bigwedge_{\alpha=n}^{N}({ct[\alpha]}\sim{\tau(\alpha)})\land\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{n}({ct[\alpha]}\sim{\tau(\alpha)-[\tau(\alpha)>0]})\land{rem}\sim{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}[\sigma(\alpha)>0]}\land(\neg(n<N)\to{n}\sim{N})

At the exit of loop (1.1), we have:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle ct[\alpha]\rangle

since counts are all equal to expressions of logical variables, so conditioning
on the logical variables, they are all deterministic; we take this formula to be
the invariant for loop (1.2). Note that the loop guard is not deterministic,
since the value of
rem
is randomized. However,
under our conditioning
,

rem
is deterministic under our conditioning since it is fully determined by
the initial counts (i.e., it is the number of buckets that are initially
non-empty). Hence, we may apply the loop rule
Loop
, treating the loop
guard as deterministic. This is the power of reasoning under conditioning.

Now to establish the invariant for loop (1.2), we reason much as in the previous
examples. The sampling rule
Samp*
gives:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle ct[\alpha]\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\langle bin[j]\rangle

By negative association for one-hot encoding (
OH-PNA
):

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle ct[\alpha]\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle bin[j][\alpha]\rangle

This implies:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle ct[\alpha]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle bin[j][\alpha]\rangle

For the inner-most loop (1.2.1), we apply the same technique as for loop (1.2).
Since loop (1.2) has a randomized guard,
k
is a random variable and loop
(1.2.1) also has a randomized guard. However, under the conditioning, we may
assume that
k
is deterministic and apply
Loop
on loop (1.2.1) with
the following invariant:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{k}\langle ct[\alpha]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\left(\langle ct[k]\rangle\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=k}^{N}\langle bin[j][\alpha]\rangle\right)\mathrel{\circledast}\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=k+1}^{N}\langle ct[\alpha]\rangle\land(\neg(k<N)\to{k}\sim{N})

Like in earlier examples, we can establish this invariant using the negative
dependence frame rule since
ct[n]+bin[j][n]
is monotone. Thus at exit of
loop (1.2.1), we have:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle ct[\alpha]\rangle

Next, three applications of the assignment rule
RAssn
give:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle ct[\alpha]\rangle\land{n}\sim{0}\land{emptyCt[r]}\sim{0}\land{empty}\sim{isZero(ct)}

The function
isZero(v)
takes a vector of numbers
v
, and returns a vector
where each index
i

1
if
v[i]
is zero, else it holds
0
. This is an
antitone function: it is non-increasing in its argument. Thus, the monotone
mapping axiom (
Mono-Map
) gives:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle empty[\alpha]\rangle

Then, a standard loop invariant for loop (1.3) gives:

\mathop{\Huge{{\circledast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle empty[\alpha]\rangle\land{emptyCt[r]}\sim{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}empty[\alpha]}

Now, we are in position to apply the negative association Chernoff bound
(
NA-Chernoff
), giving the judgment:

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{\tau(\alpha)}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{body}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[emptyCt[r]<\mathbb{E}[emptyCt[r]]-T(\rho_{empty},N)]\leq\rho_{empty}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

where
body
is the body of loop (1). However, we are not yet done. We want
to combine these judgments—one for each map
\sigma:[N]\to[N]
—using the
randomized case analysis rule
RCase

We can take the trivial pre-condition
\phi=\top
, and the case condition:

\eta=\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{\tau(\alpha)}

Since
\eta
asserts that the equality holds with probability
1
, it is closed
under conditioning. However, our post-condition has a problem: it mentions the
expected value
\mathbb{E}[emptyCt[r]]
, which may not be preserved under mixtures, so
the entire assertion is not CM. However, translating an argument by
Becchetti et al. 2019
into our logic gives:

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{\tau(\alpha)}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{body}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\mathbb{E}[emptyCt[r]]\geq N/15}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

assuming that
N\geq 2
. The argument makes use of basic properties of expected
values and the exponential function; we omit the details. Thus, we have:

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\bigwedge_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{\tau(\alpha)}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{body}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[emptyCt[r]<N/15-T(\rho_{empty},N)]\leq\rho_{empty}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

and the post-condition is now a CM assertion. Applying
RCase
, we have:

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{N}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{body}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr[emptyCt[r]<N/15-T(\rho_{empty},N)]\leq\rho_{empty}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

Recalling that we wanted the following invariant for loop (1):

\Pr\left[\bigvee_{\beta=0}^{r}(emptyCt[\beta]<T_{empty})\right]\leq r\cdot\rho_{empty}\land\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{N}

we can use the rule of constancy
Const
and the assignment rule

RAssn
to preserve the first conjunct to show:

\Pr\left[\bigvee_{\beta=0}^{r-1}(emptyCt[\beta]<T_{empty})\right]\leq(r-1)\cdot\rho_{empty}

at the end of the body of loop (1). Combined with the probability bound for

emptyCt[r]
, an application of the union bound (
UnionBd
) establishes
the invariant for loop (1). Putting everything together, we have:

\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{N\geq 2\land\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N}{ct[\alpha]}\sim{N}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}\;{\textsc{RepeatBIB}}\;\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\{$}}}}}{\Pr\left[\bigvee_{\beta=0}^{R}(emptyCt[\beta]<N/15-T(\rho_{empty},N))\right]\leq R\cdot\rho_{empty}}\hbox{\raisebox{-10.00012pt}{\resizebox{282168}{1206674}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\}$}}}}}

analogous to
Becchetti et al. 2019
.

F.5.
Axioms for Examples

For completeness, we present the probability-related axioms that we need for the
examples. For simplicity we present the axioms in binary form, though most
extend directly to big operations.

•

Linearity of expectation. Let
e,f
be
bounded
expressions.

(LinExp)

\models{\mathbb{E}[\alpha\cdot e+\beta\cdot f]}\sim{\alpha\cdot\mathbb{E}[e]+\beta\cdot\mathbb{E}[f]}

•

Union bound. Let
ev_{1},ev_{2}\in\mathcal{EV}
,

(UnionBd)

\models\Pr[ev_{1}\lor ev_{2}]\leq\Pr[ev_{1}]+\ Pr[ev_{2}]

•

Permutation marginal. Let
x
be an array variable, and let
S
be a
finite set.

(PermMarg)

\models\mathbf{Perm}_{S}\langle x\rangle\to\mathbf{U}_{S}\langle x[\alpha]\rangle

•

Expectation Indicator. Let
e
be a 0/1 valued expression,

(ExpectInd)

\models{\mathbb{E}[e]}\sim{\Pr[e=1]}

•

Bernoulli variables probabilities. Let
e
be an expression,

(BernProb)

\models\mathbf{Bern}_{p}\langle e\rangle\to\Pr[e=1]=p

•

Probability of uniform. Let
S
be a finite set.

(ProbUnif)

\models{\Pr[\mathbf{U}_{S}\langle x\rangle=\alpha]}\sim{1/|S|}

•

Bijection uniform. Let
S
be a finite set, and let
f:S\to S
be a bijection.

(BijectUnif)

\models\mathbf{U}_{S}\langle x\rangle\to\mathbf{U}_{S}\langle f(x)\rangle

•

One-hot marginal. Let
x
be an array variable.

(OHMarg)

\models\mathbf{OH}_{S}\langle x\rangle\to\mathbf{U}_{S}\langle x[\alpha]\rangle

•

Independent product one-hot.

(IndProdOH)

\models\mathbf{OH}_{[M]}\langle x\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{OH}_{[N]}\langle y\rangle\to\mathbf{OH}_{[M]\times[N]}\langle x^{\top}\cdot y\rangle

•

Independent map. Let
x
be an array variable of length
N
.

(IndMap)

\models\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle x[\alpha]\rangle\to\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{\alpha=0}^{N}\langle f(x[\alpha])\rangle

•

Deterministic independent. Let
x
be a variable.

(DetInd)

\models\mathbf{Detm}\langle x\rangle\to\langle x\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\langle e\rangle

•

Events happen only if they have probability one. Let
ev\in\mathcal{EV}
,

(ProbOne)

\models{ev}={1}\to\Pr(ev)=1

•

Uniform sampling from a population.
We represent a population as a bit-vector, where each entry is an individual
and 1 indicates they have some feature and 0 indicates not. Then, if we
uniformly sample from the population, the probability of getting a one is
equal to population-level ratio of ones, regardless how they are distributed
in the population. Let
N\geq J
be constants or logical variables,
b
be an
array variable of length
N
, and
x,hit
be variables:

(UniformSamp)

\models\left(\left(\mathbf{bv}(b,J,N)\mathrel{\ast}\mathbf{U}_{[N]}\langle x\rangle\right)\land{hit}\sim{b[x]}\right)\to\mathbf{Bern}_{hit}\langle\frac{J}{N}\rangle\mathrel{\ast}\left(\sum_{\beta=0}^{N}b[\beta]=J\right).

•

Independent product probabilities. Let
ev_{1},ev_{2}\in\mathcal{EV}
,

J,K
be two real numbers,

(IndepProb)

\models\Pr[ev_{1}]\leq J\mathrel{\ast}\Pr[ev_{2}]\leq K\to\Pr[ev_{1}\land ev_{2}]\leq J\cdot K.

•

Equal probabilities.
Let
b_{1},b_{2}
be two boolean expressions. Recall that
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathcal{EV}
too.

(EqualProb)

\models{b_{1}}\sim{b_{2}}\to\Pr[b_{1}]=\Pr[b_{2}]
